LADIES' LETTER

MY DEAREST NELLIE, Braid is, without a doubt, the most fashionable trimming for day gowns and mantles, and with a little care it may be manipulated successfully by the merest novice among amateur dressmakers. Narrow braid is, of course, much eabier to arrange than that of the broader description. A litble care will rectify any little blunder that might be made. Supposing you intend to run plain bands of braid round your dress skirt, you must so arrange that youo ommence and finish at the centre back seaw. Leave a good inch over at either end of the braid, and push this inside the seam, tacking all firmly together on the wrong aide.

Velvet Is of the most fashion- able materials at the prestnt time, and the rieh shades which I have seen on every hand has suggested•their adaptability for millinery. A little toque of the TAM o' Shanter type, with ostrich tips and osprey of the same shade as the velvet, together with a band of sable, a steel buckle and violets would be alike becoming to a girl not quite out of her teens, and to a young lady a few years older, an ordinary turhin may be used as a foundation for this headgear, and I may say that carried out in a pale shade of velvet, such as peach pink, daffodil yellow, or lime green, with dater brown or black feathers, shaded or tipped to correspond or self-coloured, as preferred, with the band either of sable, mink, or beaver, it is difficult to conceive a smarter chapeau for either theatre or concert wear.

A ilainty and useful evening gown with Retrained nei, ltirt is trimmed bewitchingly with bows and bads of the now, fashionable ribbon. The bodice takes a line of its own, and in no way carries out the design employed on the skirt. Cut with the new full basque and open, zouave-like fronts, this bodice could be worn over various skirts, and shou:d prove a useful addition to any wardrobe. For the colour of this bodice, deep shades only are admissible; and, if you take my advice, you will decide in favour of black, which is so useful and so becoming. The model is edged with 'fur, which forms a charming &deb, but, as this is arranged in so prominent a position, it must be good—which, you know, is only another word for expensive. Therefore, to those of you who cannot afford a length of really good fur, I say substitute a jet edging, or oven a lace frill. If the latter, it must be arranged to fall over the shoulders and bust.

Walking toilettes are now either of the very glorified kind, or elae form a contrast by their very simplicity. For church parade the pelisse reigns supreme, and is generally of velvet and satin, trimmed with rich fur; or, if cloth is used, it is of the handsomest type, and covered with braiding and beading, and combined with velvet and fur. To be fashionable no colourings can be too bright; but in better taste are the dark violets, petunias, and olive-greens, trimmed ,with jet or black sequins and dark fur,

A charming walking costume I have seen recently is of more elaborate design than usual. The entire dress is of rich black onduline silk. The bodice, out with deep basque, is turned back with wide revers of black velvet, to display a folded vest of white Bengaline. The entire costume is edged with wolverine fur, thus forming a visiting gown of unusual elegance. The smart toque is of black velvet edged with wolverine, finished at the back with a small bow of white velvet, and in the front with a small blaok jetted bird and some jetted and white osprey.

A season9.ble coat is of thick tan-coloured box cloth, plushed on the wrong side, and further lined throughout with a dainty silk lining. It is trimmed round the neck and shoulders with a handsome skunk collar, which continues in a nar- row stripe down the front edges of the coat, turn- ing, however, ere the bottom is reached, and with a graceful curve continues round the sides in a novel manner. Such a coat looks best worn over a plain skirt of cloth or tweed, while a smart finish is obtained by a small Dutch bonnet of petunia-coloured velvet, edged with fur, and trimmed back and front with rosettes of the velvet each holding an osprey of jet. Strings of petunia way be added; but these little bonnets are con- tinually worn without 41T1ngs of any description. Yours loving friend, JENNIE.

When the great violinist, Dr. Joachim, pays his an- nual visit to this country next month, he will he pro- bably accompanied by his daughter, Frualein Joachim, who will then make her first appearance in England as a singer.

AS all the mayors of New Zealand are ex-officie jus- tices of the peace, Mrs. Yates, who has just been elected chief magistrate of Onehunza, will thus be the first female magistrate in the British Empire, as well as the first woman mayor.

Only once a year nowadays, and then on the occasion of a religious festival, does the Empress of Japan appear in public in native dress. Ordinarily she attires herself very handsomely after the style of Worth, of Paris, and a correspondent reports having seen her in a costume of blue velvet trimmed with chinchilla, with a dainty Parisian bonnet of blue velvet on her head. Any woman with an average figure might, says a gossiping correspondent, just now get the respectable homage of the eyes of men by appearing in a slender- shouldered jacket and graceful petticoat. But she will not. She prefers the approval of a dressmaker to the looks of lovers. A London optician made, last year, a unique depar- ture in the mounts of opera glasses. He produced a pair studded with turquoises and pearls as a wedding gift for the Duchess of York. A well-known London banker has now " gone one better," having ordered through a city firm a pair with the rims of the object glass cells studded with diamonds, the mounting being mother-o'-pearl and aluminium. The price of the- binocular is £l2O. One icy night Charlotte Cushman and Lawrence Bar rett came out of the theatre together. The steps were dangerously slippery, and it was with difficulty that they kept their feet at all. As they totteringly descend:d, the great actress said to her companion, quite in her Lady Macbeth manner, "Take a good grip on my arm, Lawrence, and if I slip hold on like grim death; but if you slip, in the name of heaven let go." _ There is sure to be a rush upon Girton and Newnham now that an earl's daughter has declared her intention of becoming a sweet girl graduate at the former college, says the "Princess." The hearts of the Cambridge undergraduates will, I am afraid, be most terribly flut- tered by the presence of the lovely-faced Lady Ulrica Duncombe in their midst. All the Earl of Feversham's daughters are beautiful, and the lately widowed young Duchess of Leinster especially so ; but the general opinion in society is that the youngest, Lady Ulrica, is the fairest of all. The Duchess of Hamilton, besides being a devotee to sport, is one of the great ladies who are fond of playing dairymaid in practical fashion. She has built herself a beautiful little private dairy at Easton Park. The walls are entirely of marble the floors terra-cotta, and the tables pure white alabaster. In the centre there is a marble fountajn of the purest spring water. Her Grace often gets up soon after daybreak, and makes dainty little pats of butter ready to appear at the breakfast- table. Sometimes she even does the milking.

The Eyes,

When the eyes are treated fairly they are strengthened not weakened, by work. Just as the arms of a black- smith grow the stronger for his trade, so the eyes of watchmakers who work under healthy conditions are found to improve and not to deteriorate in vigour and quickness. It is the abuse of the eyes, nOt their use, Which is to be avoided. If a man is aware either that his eyes need no artificial correction or else have received their proper adjustment, and if his work, whether liter- ary or mechanical, is done in a light both steady and sufficient and with a due regard to ordinary sanitary rules, he may feel sure that he is strengthening his eyes, not weakening them by hard work.

Coffee. Flavouring.

A flavouring of coffee is one of the most delicious of the new flavourings. It may be used in custards, ice- creams, the icing of cake, and in many other cold desserts. A charlotte russe flavoured with coffee is especially nice. To flavour a quart of custard, take two heaping tablespoonfuls of Mocha coffee, ground quite fine and measured alter grinding. If possible,the coffee should have been browned and ground the day it is used. Pour the quart of milk of which the custard is to be made boiling hot over the coffee, beat the whole thor- oughly for two or three minutes over the fire. Then remove it from the stove, cover it and let it stand where it will gradually cool, and the flavouring of the coffee will %-x infused through the milk. Then strain it. This gives a delicate, delicious flavour of coffee.

Wadded Skirts. The bell-shaped skirt is to once again obtain amongst us in the spring, and we have realised how to cut this to perfection. By we, you will understand, please, the best artists are implied, for it is far without the ken of the ordinary dressmaker even now. To wad her skirt is one of the first duties of the woman fashionable, says "Hearth and Home," and this is rather difficult to man- age ; unless the wadding be sewn very neatly, and very extensively, it has a fatally unpleasant habit of settling itself into lumps. The skirts are wadded at the back and round the hems, this inducing weight but to a very slight degree, and, strange as it may seem, this method is adopted alike on the tweed gown and the silken costame which shall pass its hour at the dineeriparty;or the dance.

Preservation of Cut Flowers.

The mistake is ver • often made of exposing cut flowers in rooms to sharp currents of air ; thus, if a stand is arranged and placed near a wind3w that is open a-considerable part of the day, the flowers and foli- age must of necessity fade the sooner. This caution is the more requisite when we have to do with forced flowers early in the spring ; these, by reason of being pressed into flower out of their proper season, have not, as a rule, that persistency of power of resisting this ex- posure which flowers have in their natural season. The same caution is required in dealing with tender exotics, during chilly weather especially. The better way will be to place the flowers in a position where this evil can be partially avoided, yet not in proximity to a fire-place. Lut flowers are oftentimes placed in small vases on the chimney piece ; this when the fire is alight is a great mistake, worse even than th 6 exposure to the draught. In lieu of using fresh flowers in such a position,we would urge all decorators to furnish their vases with everlast- ing flowers. With very choice flowers on whieh special value may be placed, it is an excellent plan to arrange them in a vase which when filled can be covered with a glass shade. In this manner their retention of colour and freshness will be greatly enhanced. When each vase in its turn has to be refilled, some of the flowers, if necessary, can gecerally be used over again, excepting in the summer months, when the quantity is more abun- dant. Those flowers that are still fresh should have a little piece taken off the end of the stem, so that the fresh water given them is the more readily absorbed. The water in which stocks in particular, and some other flowers also, are placed, is seen rendered obnoxious ; in such cases fresh supplies should be given at least every other day, the vases at the same time having a thorough cleansing.—" Cassell's Popular Gardening."

_ A MILKMAN'S DODGE.

The Farmers and Cleveland Dairy Company, who em- ploy some four hundred milk sellers, have for some time been aware that a system Nf adulterating their milk with water had been going on for some time in Barns- ley. Having set a watch they discovered that one of their men was carrying two cans, one a large and the other a small one, and on the detective asking to be servcd with a sample it was observed that the Company's servant was particularly anxious to draw it from a larger vessel. The detective, however, preferred the smaller one, which led to the discovery that the latter contained a weak, diluted milk which the man had been u‘ing for mixing with the better quality. What was left unsold in both cans was, it appeared brought back and credited to the man. Nine pints of water were found to have been introduced to make up for a large quantity of milk or its value appropriated. The dishon- est milkman has been convicted and fined forty shillings and costs.

PARTED BY FATE;

OR THE MYSTERY OF BLACK-TOR LIGHTHOUSE. BY LAURA JEAN LIBBEY, Author of "lone, the Pride of the Mill," &c., &o.

CHAPTER RXXV.

A FATAL MISTAKE.

Beautiful, unfortunate Uldene's grief was all the more poignant because she loved Rutledge so well; yet there was a reason, strong as life itself, and more bitter than death, why this hapless daughter, the last of an accursed race, dare not share Rutledge Chester's home and heart.

"Oh, God, what shall I do? What way shall I turn?" she repeated, wringing her hands and raising her pale face to the pitiless night winds that drifted in with the moonlight through the open window. In the solemn hush of midnight strange thoughts came to her. Why should Verlie be happy in his love while she was doomed to the darkness of cold despair? It would not be so hard for Verlie to give him up as for her to lose him beyond recall—forever. Why not go to Verlie, make herself known to her, bind her to a vow of eternal silence, and tell her all? Under the pitiful circumstances, Verlie would certainly see that she must never reveal herself to Rutledge. How she would kneel at Verlie'e feet and beg of her not to hate hr for separating them. Verlie was sweet and good as an angel. Uldene knew that she would clasp her arms around her, and say, gently: "Do not grieve me, Uldene, darling. I will give him up to you without a murmur, since it is God's will. You are restored to us from the very grave; that will be recompense and solace enough for me." Ah, yes, she must see Viten° and tell her all. She had no time to lose if she would save her. Uldene knew that her new-found friend would object most strenuously to her leaving the cottage in bar present weak condition. She must steal cautiously away without her knowledge. Yes, that would he bast.

Leaving a note to her, carefully pinned to the white pillow, in which she expressed her heartfelt gratitude for the kindness that had been shown her, and hoping the time mtght come when she oeuld repay tenfold that kindness, Uldene stole .gently away from the humble cottage' and from the kind woman who had indeed pro the friend in her hour of greatest need. Uldene, faint and weak, pushed steadily on through the intense darkness which preceded the dawn, reaching the railway station as the faint pink streak of early morning was tinting the gray of the eastern sky. She was just in time. The southern express was within five minutes of starting. Uldene pur- chased her ticket, entered the carriage, and took her seat. The sun rose in a flood of crimson and gold glory, travelled through the blue, hazy, cloudless sky, reached the zenith, passed it, and sank slowly to- ward the west. .Still the train whirled on past green fields, small villages, woodland and stream. The dusk settled down, and the night crept on, and One by one the golden stars fixed themselves in the blue sky, and in the darkness of the night the train I stopped at Uldenets destination. Gathering her cloak closely about her, and drawing her veil down over her face, Uldene stepped from the ear. The wharf was But a few steps distant. She made her way to it Without delay, looking furtively around for the long, weather-beaten building, or shed rather, that had always stood to the right of it, when, little boats could be hired. It was not there now; it had been either torn down or the high tide had swept it away. "A .. t, min? Was you looking for a boat?" .. a voice at her elbow. "Yea," answered Uldene, anxiously. "I want to get over to Black-Tor Light-House. I do not need any one to row me over. I—l can handle the oars myself," she added, placing the usual fee in the man's hand. "They are going to have a great time over to the old light-house to-night,' said the boatman. "There's to be a grand wedding. Many a load of merry young folks I've carried over in my boat to-day." "When is the ceremony to take place? At what hour, do you know ?" asked Uldene, trembling in every limb. "At ten, prompt. That is what I heard," he answered. At that moment the clock in an adjoining steeple tolled the hour of eight. Uldene took the oars in her white hands, and the little skiff shot out like al! arrow into the blue, dancing waves. Once a desperate feeling came over her to fling herself from the little boat down, down into the silvery, star-tipped waves, and let the marriage go on. Then a great thrill of joy filled her heart that it was in her power to prevent any other woman from being the bride of him whom she loved better than life itself. At last the keel of the little boat touched the sand of the small island, and Uldene sprang ashore, drawing her boat into safety amongst the dense overhanging willows, and securing it to an ircin staple. This accomplished, she was about to turn to- wards the house, when a hurried, cautious, creep- ing footstep fell upon her ear, and an instant later the tall figure of a man emerged from the path iato sight. The clear moonlight fell upon his dark, sinister Lc; revealing to Uldene the face of her mortal foe—the man who knew the horrible secret of the daughttrs of her race, and who had forced her to fly from her husband, if she would not have that story blazoned to the world. "What was he doing here 7" Uldene asked her- self, breathless with horror. He stopped short, and gazed up at the brilliantly illuminated towers with a tierce light in his ()ark, threatening eyes. He was se near Uldene she sould have reached out her hand and touched hint from where she crouched in such abject terror amongst the trees. She was in deadly fear lest he would hear the loud beating of her heart. But no; he was too deeply engrossed in his own thoughts for that. " liow strange it is, Rutledge Chester, that your path and mine should have crossed again I" he muttered, aloud. "in wedding your first bride you would have robbed me of my wealth, that would have gone to her on her ei;.l eenth birthday had she lived ; and in we Itling this bride you win from me the only woman I have ever loved in all my reckless, sin-hardened-life—a reckless life, handed down from lather to son. From the day you learned, through accident, that I—the trusted bank cashier—was a forger, a smdggler and a gambler, and blozoned it out to the world, so that ever since the officers of the law have been on my track. I have sworn to be avenged upon you. You shall not marry Verlie Sefton to-night! I swear it! I would see you lying dead at my feet first! Ah there is Rutledge Chester now 1" he exclaimed, as a tall figure emerged from the open door, and strolled down the path, directly, toward the dark, shadowy pines behind which the des- perado had stepped. . In that instant Uldene realised Rutledge's peril. There would be a fearful tragedy, uhl, ss she pre- vented it. Rutledge, whom she loved Letter than life itself, wound be slain. Ah, heaven help her to save him!

She tried to cry out to warn him, but her ttngne clove to the roof of her mouth; the wild cry died on her lips, making no sound, and the blood setmod freem in. her _veins with nfroktitl teiror. Her

lirnbs refused 'o hold her slight weight, and she sank down among the sharp brambles; but in that awful moment of speechless terror she bad seen a horrible sight. The tall figure had stepped abreast of the trees—then the hidden foe had sprung upon him. There was a sharp gleam of steel in the moonlight, and Uldene knew no more. In an instant a thrilling cry had broken the still- ness of the night air, and startled the gay group strolling down the sand not far distant, and im- mediately they hurried to the spot from whence the sound had proceeded. And in that fatal moment another small skiff grated on the sand, and Captain Lansing, pale and haggard, but resolute, sprang out. "I am sorry that I did not accept Rutledge's overtures towards friendship," he mused. " Ah, well, if Verlie, the dear girl, can be happier with him than she could have been with me, I bow to the decree of fate. It will be a great surprise to both Verlie and Rutledge to see that I have accepted their kind invitation—even at the last moment—to be present at the wedding. I have had a hard fight with my heart, but, thank God, good feeling has conquered. I—" "Hello 1 What's this ?" he cried aloud.

He hd stumbled against something lying across the path—a human body. "I was not mistaken, then. I did hear a terrible cry a moment since," he muimured, bending down over the prostrate form. "Has there been a tragedy here?" In that moment strong hands seized him. There was a babel of voices—a flashing of lights. The grounds, in a single instant, were filled with frightened guests, who thronged around the pros- trate figure, looking askance at the captain, and whispering, with bated breath, as they closed in around him.

It was not Rutledge Chester who had met such a narrow escape from a terrible fate, but a young man who, unfortunately, closely resembled him. He had not fainted; the fearful blow struck by the unknown assassin had glanced aside, just above his heart, making but a slight incision, and, faint and breathless, he was explaining how he had come down the path, when from behind the pines a man had sprung out, and a hoarse voice had cried out: "You shall never marry Verlie Sefton, the only woman I have ever loved. You shall die first, Rutledge Chester.' Then followed the fatal blow which had so nearly cost him his life.

The throng fell back and glanced, in dismay, at the pale, determined face of the handsome captain, and at Rutledge himself, who Was standing there, with horror expressed on his countenance too deep for words.

There were many in that group who knew that Rutledge Chester and the handsome captain had been bitter rivals for Verlie Sefton's love. They knew, too, of the reckless threat of vengeance the captain had uttered. They had heard the fatal vow that had burst so thoughtlessly from his lips in a moment of madness—that he would wrest her from his rival at the very altar. The horror-stricken throng gazed at each other with expressive glances and low whispered words.

"My friends—you, Rutledge—can you believe this was my work ?" asked the young captain, aghast. "I swear to you lam innocent. I own that, up to to-night, I had bitter feelings against you; but on this, your wedding night, I came to ask you to pardon my hasty words and my ill- feeling, and to beg for the friendship that, a fortnight since, I scornfully rejected. Do you believe me 2"

Rutledge Chester was too shocked to find words in which to answer him. And those who gathered around the wounded man, to bear him up to the lighthouse, turned their faces from him with darkening brows, in which he read his answer. Suddenly, through the shocked group, a little slender figure burst—a little slim figure in white surah silk and dark, curling hair. It was poor Neddy. "Though the world should believe you guilty and turn from you, I should still believe in your innocence, Captain Lansing," she cried out, falteringly. "I know you have not done this awful deed. Why don't somebody search the grounds ? " Among all of Captain Lansing's friends—many of whom had known him from his boyhood up— only this one young girl stood by him, believing him guiltless. He was a brave soldier and a haughty man, but tears came to his eyes—tears of emotion and gratitude—as he looked down upon her. "Thank you for your faith in me, Miss Temple," he said, brokenly, bending his handsome head over the little, extended fluttering white hand, and clasping it closely. "Your words have given me courage to face this terrible accusation and prove my innocence. I shall never rest with such a cloud darkening my fair name and fame. I shall fiud out who gild this deed committed to- night, if it costs every cent of my fortune." A sturdy official—one of Mark Sefton's old fziends, who had been invited to the wedding as one of the guests, never dreaming a sterner duty would be required of him—here stepped forward. "It is my painful duty to arrest you on sus- picion, Mr.-7 Mr.—"

"Lansing," supplemented the captain, bowing gravely. "I submit, sir," he answered, proudly. "I shall not interfere in the performance of your duty." And amidst Neddy's hysterical weeping and the murmur cf his old friends—who had turned against him in his hour of need—the poor captain was led away. And while this conclusion was going on in the grounds a pitiful scene was being enacted in the bride-elect's chamber. A dark figure had glided In through the open doorway of the lighthouse, swiftly ascending the spiral stairway, pausing breathlessly before the door of 7erlie's room, push- ing it open with unsteady, trembling hands. "Is it you, Neddy ?" exclaimed Verlie, turning suddenly around. "What its the world is happen- ing out there in the grounds? You promised to come back instantly and let me know." As she spoke, she lifted her blue eyes, behold- ing, not gay, laughing Neddy, but a slim figure dressed in a (lark travelling dress, bedraggled with dew and sea-weed, her face heavily shrouded by a thick, black veil. We will pass lightly over the scenes that fol. lowed, dear reader, during the long weeks that Verlie Sefton lay sick unto death, oblivious of all that was transpiring around her. Those terrible weeks an honourable man—who was innocent as a babe of the crime of which he was accused—spent in a prison oelL There was a horrible chain of -circumstantial evidence around the poor captain that would drag him down to his doom. Fate itself seemed to have tightened its merciless web about him. The tide of I,opular opinion and feeling was against him. He knew that he was not the first man who had been a martyr to circumstantial evidence, he told himself, bitterly. He was a Lansing, and the Lansings were all brave men; he would not be the first of the race who had been called upon to face a trying ordeal. r. Poor Noddy How bravely she tried to inspire him with hope, even up to the last. There was but one person who could have saved him, who listened attentively to all that was said of the pitiful case, in the little Adjacent village to which she had fled, easily concealing her identity behind a widow's cap and blue glasses, and that was Uldene herself.

One word from her would prove him guiltless of the terrible charge, but in doing this she would be obliged to reveal her own identity, and from this Uldene shrank in pitiful terror. She was dead to Rutledge and the world; she must never reveal herself; even Verlie, who had heard her terrible story, had agreed—for Rutledge's sake—it must be so.

She was the only person who could point out the true would-be assassin, and clear this young captain's name and fame. Should she do it, and suffer the consequences that would inevitably follow, or not? Long and earnestly Uldene pondered over the thought. If she set the officers of the law upon the daring stranger's track, and they should hunt him down, he would take a horrible vengeanoe upon her by blazoning her history and her story to the scandat-loving world. And yet because of this fatal power he held over her, should she let the in- nocent man suffer?

_____------ . Uldene was sorely perplexed. She wan so young, so friendless, with no one to advise—no one to reason with her—it is not to be wondered that she hesitated so pitifully. • The days and weeks flew by, bringing at last the fatal day of Captain Lansing's trial. The court room was crowded to its utmost eapaeity. Everyone pitied the handsome young prisoner in the box; yet, in the face of the net- work of circumstantial evidence around him, who could believe him guiltless? The captain and Rutledge Chester had been bitter rivals for a fair lady's love. Rutledge's wooing had met with success—the captain's with ignominious defeat, and he had sworn a terrible oath of vengeance upon his more fortunate rival, vowing that the fair lady should never be his bride, for he would snatch her from him at the very altar. As the fatal testimony of one after another was given, brave as the captain was. cold dew stood out on his brow and trickled down on his clenched hands. His lips were set in a straight tense line that betokened the keenest agony, and tears that were no 'disgrace to his manhood sprang to his eyes. A prison cell for a long term of years stared him in the face. Better death than that. He realised with his quick intuition, when he saw the faces of the jury turned away from him, as they filed slowly back to their seats, what the verdict would be. He was innocent; yet on circumstantial evidence he would be couvieted.

" Gentle Men," said the judge, solemnly addressing the jury, "have you found a verdict?' The foreman rose slowly to his feet, facing the judge. There was a great hush in the vast assem- blage. Every one bent breathlessly forward to hear what followed. Every one anticipated what his answer must be.

"Your honour," answered the foreman, sorrow- fully, yet with terrible distinctness, "we, the jury, find the prisoner gui—" The terrible word was never finished. A slender figure heavily veiled, rose up from among the crowd with a piercing cry, and gasped out; "Hold, your honour I I have something to say !" and as she spoke, Uldene—for it was she— flung the veil back from her face, and faced the horror-stricken throng. SAD DEATH OF A LIVERPOOL CAPTAIN AT RUNCORN.

On Monday at the Seamen's Institute Runcorn, Mr. J. E. Worsley held sin inquest on the body of Captain Frederick Charles -Wallandaine, of Rock Ferry, overlooker for Messrs. Price and Company of Liverpool who died on board the sailing ship "Queen of the West" at Runcorn Docks on Saturday from injuries receive& the previous Sunday. Mr. Francis Wiswall C.E. of Stanley Villas, watched the proceedings on behalf of the relatives of the de- ceased; and Mr. John F. Jellico of Liverpool was present for the Scottish Metropolitan Life Assu ance Company, in which society the deceased was insured for £l,OOO. Inspector Cooper acted for the police. Mr. Wiswall who said he was cousin by marriage, gave evidence of identification. The deceased was 55 years of age, and was a strong healthy man.— William Griffiths first mate on the Queen of the West, "said the deceased came on Board at six o'clock on Saturday evening the 20th ult. He appeared in his usual health and made no complaint of feeling ill. The ship began to drag her anahor and witness went forward to help in dropping her anchor. When this was done, he found the deceased sitting on the poop. He said he was awfully bad ; he had slipped on the deck and he suffered from rheumatics. He helped him into the cabin. The accident on the quarter deck had not caused the wound on the deceased's forehead. Gertrude Canadine, of the Nurses' Institute, 70, Hope-street, Liverpool, said she was called in to attend to the deceased a week age. He was lying on a bed made upon the table in the cabin in the vessel which was lying in Runcorn Dock. On Tues- day night he regained consciousness for a short time and told her that he slipped on the deck on Saturday night, but he did not say that he hurt himself then. He then went down into the cabin and fell asleep on the locker. He was falling from there and got hold of the stove to save himself. The stove was hot, and he let go and then fell with his head against the sharp corner of the form. He severely burned his right hand.—Dr. Murdock MaaKenzie said he attended the deceased from the time of vessel arrivinglin Runcorn until his death. In his opinion the cause of death was concussion of the brain. the accident as described to the nurse was such as would be likely to cause the injuries from which the deceased suffered. The steward who had witnessed the accident had joined another ship, but be had left a written statement. The Coroner there was no occasion for taking that statement at that in- quiry.—Mr. Jellico asked whether it was probable the , faintness of giddiness caused the fall or an accidental , slip.—Dr. MacKenzie said he thought it was accidental fall. The statement of the steward supported this view. In reply to Mr. Williams (a juryman), Dr. MacKenzie said he did not think the fall on the deck bad anything to do with the concussion. If it had he would have been unconscious all the time and it would have taken seven or eight mee to have carried him below. He was a very big man.—The Coroner said there could be very little doubt that it was a very lamentable and sad accident. They had it from his own mouth how it had occurred. The jury returned a verdict of "accidental death."

COMPLICATED MARRIAGE.

Marriage in Sikkim and Thibet, according to Babu Sarat Chandra Das—who has been reading papers on this subject before the Bengal Asiatic Society—is a serious matter for bridegrooms, for the rites and cere- m.inies are both many and costly. Formerly the festi- vities were kept up for nine days and nights. They now last three days, and end with what is called the "dance of marriage," in which partner pulls his partner's ears. The guests then disperse, and the married couple retire. In ' Puran, it seems, the custom of marriage by capture is still in 'force. The lady is "deported," and well looked after until such time as an alliance can be nego- tiated. If the parents are hostile, or if the heroine is refractory, the matter is .laid before the village elders, who have apparently power to overrule objections, and fix an auspicious day for the wedding. "When a girl," says this authority. " has given her heart to a man,but her parents will net let her marry him, she elopes with him." "Marriage by elopement,' however, seems to be accompanied by tedious formalities. The bride having been conveyed to a safe place, the father and other rela- Lives of the lover, loaded with gifts and provisions, set forth to appease the angry parents. At a distance of a hundred yards from the house, they take up their posi- tion and wave a khatag, or "salutation scarf:" to inti- mate their avowal of an offer of marriage—a mode of procelure which has been described as "popping the question by flag signal." At first the parents take no notice of this, and decline to look at them ; but the " longmi " continue their entreaties for three or four days, and do not leave the place until by their impor- tunity they have moved the hearts of the bride's par- ents.

MILITARY TOURNAMENT AND ASSAULT AT ARMS AT EARLESTOWN.

Under the auspices of the Earlestown, Newton and District Gymnasium, and in aid of the funds a grand tournament aid assault at arms was given in the Town Hall,Earlestown, on Thursday evening before a large and fashionable audience,by members of the local gymnasium, the Liverpool gymnusiuzi. "C " Troop of Lancashire hussars, and members of the Newton Campany of the 6 c. V.B.K.L. R., under the direction of Mr. R. Brunton. Colonel J. L. Wood presided and there were also present the Hon. T. W. Legh, M.P., Robert Stone, J.P., F. J. Wood, T. D. Grimke-Drayton, John Stone, Dr. Stone, Dr. Street, R. Stuart, and others. Colonel Wood, in opening the proceedings, said he was glad to see that notwithstanding the unfavourable state of the weather such a splendid audience had assembled in the new Town Hall to witness a display by members of the gymnasium. Their presence in such large num- bers, would, he was sure, encourage the members, to give their exercises with greater spirit than they would have done if the hall had only been half filled. (Hear, hear.) Their attendance in such large numbers also showed their interest in the physical training of young men. The noble records of victories by this country, were owing entirely to the sports and pastimes which had been in force for ages past. In our own district we had snorts and pastimes of whlch we ought to be proud but time would not permit him to go into details, he, however, would mention the games of golf, cricket and football. Speaking directly of the object of that gather- ing, he said there was a lack of a suitable room for young people to meet where they could receive instruc- tion in physical training. He understood Mr. Brunton to say that this want had to a considerable extent been met, and they would that night see what a few months cf instruction had done. A considerable sum of money had been subscribed, but there was much more wanted to complete and fit up a building, and on behalf of the Committee and members of that desirable institution he appealed te outside friends to help forward this good work, and so assist in the establishment of what must be from a moral and physical point of view, a great benefit to the young men of the neighbourhood. (Applause.) The exercises were then proceeded with, the first item being sword exercises by a squad of the Lancashire hussars, under the instruction of Sergeant Major Strange. Then followed a competition by members of the local gymnasium, including, barbells, dumb bells, and Indian Clubs, for six silver medals; bayonet exercise by squad of the 8. T.8.L.R., under, the instruction of Sergeant Instructor Sweeney, and physical drill by the same squad ; lance exercises by the "B " troop of hussars, [under the instruction of Sergeant Major Cook ; boxing,. high jumping, in which Mr. Dobson of Liverpool cleared sft. 4in., on a slippery floor. Sword feats by Sergeant- Major Slorrer, including splitting a potatoe on the hand, neck and throat of a man. The members of the Liver- pool Gymnasium, also gave some clever exhibitions on 'f• the parr allel bars, in which Messrs. Lunn, Mason, Dobson, Singleton, Williams, Tickers, and - Davies, tonic part, their performances being loudly ap-- plauded. Their display also included rope-climbing, which was much admired. The prizes for the competitions by the local gymnasts were Messrs. Lunn and Mason—(Liverpool)—and the prizes were awarded as follows :-1, J. Miller, (57) marks 2J. Liptrot (55) 3 • J. Cooke, (53) A. Shallcross, (52), W. Gee, 51, and W. Speakman (50), and medals were presented accordingly by the chairman, who also announced that the competi- tors had only had 12 lessons. A fencing display by Dr. - Street and Sergeant Major Slorrer was also much ad- mired. Mr. T. W. Legh, in proposing a vote of thanks to the chairman, congratulated him and the members upon an unqualified success. The proceedings had been both interesting and encouraging they had been privileged to. witness the kind of feats in which Englishmen liked to. excel, and also feats under trying circumstances on the part of their friends. It was rather late to talk about the advantages of a gymnasium for a district like that. The advantages were so obvious that it was not neces- sary to say much on the subject. It was an object deserving the support of all individuals, and after that meeting he was certain that sue- cess of the project was assured. Before long a gymnasium in that locality would be established, and he had no doubt himself that that district would be able to furnish a thoroughly efficient team of athletes who would be some day probably able to compete with such tried performers as the Liverpool gymnasium, whese performances had given them so much pleasure that night. (Applause.) Mr. T. D. Grimke-Drayton seconded, and it was heartily accorded.

Mr. Robert Stone moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Brun- ton, the members of the Liverpool Gymnasium, the volunteers and hussars, and Mr. R. Stuart having seconded, it was unanimously accorded.

THE HON. T. W. LEGH, M.P., AT EARLES- TOWN.

Mr. Legh the member for the Newton division was present at a meeting of the Conservative Asso- ciation in the Town Hall, Newton, on Saturday afternoon. Representatives were present from all parts of the division. Mr. Legh in a few remarks expressed the pleasure it gave him to meet such a representative gathering, which showed that they had th,t unionist cause at heart. The Hon. gentleman afterwards paid a visit to the Earlesto cvn Conservative Club, where a smoking concert was being held. Mr. C. B. F. Borron presided and Mr. Robert Stone waci also present.

The Chairman said it gave him great pleasure to be present, the more so because they had Mr. Legh their member with them.

Mr. Legh was not aware that the concert was to have been held that evening, otherwise he would have made arrangements to stay the evening, but he had to return on the next train. He would therefore with their per- mission, call upon him to say a few words, and give them an oppoortunity of hearing his voice, which they had not heard this year. He extended to him hearty wishes for a happy new year. (Applause.) Mr. Legh who was received with loud cheers, said he came there to attend a meeting of the association, and he had no idea that such an entertainment was in con- templation. He supposed he was expected to say some- thing, as members of Parliament usually were. Some members talked a lot, but said nothing. There were various topics that occurred to hirmbut he did not think that any of them had any connection with a smoking concert, he did not think the question of Parish Councils or the Employers' Liability would be appropriate. He had read in the papers that two distinguished statesmen, had lately been addressing their con- stituents, but these gentlemen could not find anything particular to talk about, how much more diffi- cult then must it be for him to enchain their attention during the few minutes he had to stay. But he supposed he must say a word or two on the political situation. During the greater part of last year, in fact almost the whole of the year, he had sat as their representative,and there was very little to show for it, and if he had been in receipt of a salary there would be very little to show for the money. What was there to show ? As far as he could make out, it amounted to this, they had sat the whole of last year,with the exception of about six weeks , for the one purpose of picking a quarrel with the House 'of Lords, and the secret process was with the object of catching votes. The next general election wouldecer- tainly not take place until the last possible moment, as far as the Government was concerned. At the present time the House of Commons were endeavouring to force a quarrel upon minor subjects. The two questions upon which the two houses were likely to fall out at the pre- -sent time, were tl-le Parish Council Bill, and the Em- ployers' Liability Bill. Be had received a petition, which he had been asked to present, signed by 1100 workmen, in favour of being able to contract out of the Employers' Liability Bill. It remained to be seen what course would be taken by the Lords in this particular bill. John Burns, and other labour leaders had told them that the workingmen were not in favour of contracting out, but he (Mr. Legh) was in favour of liberty of action. It would only be logical and fair, if the House of Lords stuck to the clause, and allowed men to contract out of the bill, if they so desired. (Hear, hear.) Supposing the two houses did come into conflict, he was of opinion that the House of Lords would be backed up by the country, as one man. It would take a good deal to rouse the country against the House of Lords, and although it was not representative, it at the present moment pos- sessed the confidence 01 the country more than it had done in the previous portion of its existence. England was with them in the rejection of the Home Rule BIN and the great majority of the country would always be in favour of the Lei ds. He would like to know whe was the better for the present Government, or who had obtained any benefit from it ? The only credit they could claim was their intervention in the coal strike, a step which should have been taken earlier. The Concili- ation Board might be a success, but it was just as well not to anticipate too much from boards of that charac- ter, they inuit not be too sanguine as to the result. It gave him great pleasure to be there, as it always did. That club had always been looked upon as the strong- hold of the party, and it was to them to whom they looked for assistance when the struggles took place, and they did not look in vain. (Appla.use.) Mr. Legh then retired, and the concert was proceeded with, ari excellent programme of glees, songs, and reci- tations being given by Messrs. George Wright, Jamee Nichols, C. H. Los, W Beatty, W. Mawdsley, G. Bailey, W.Stitton and T. Bowes. Mr.E. Leyland accompanied at the piano. On tie proposition of Mr. Borron, a vote of thanks to Mr. Legli, seconded by Mr. S;one was enthusiasticallY accorded, and a similar compliment was paid to the Chairman.

It stated that the sum of £65,000 is still required to deal thoroughly with the distress caused by the failure cf the Balfour companies.

There are few men in America so well known as Dr. Talmage of the Brooklyn Tabernacle. He weekly ad- dresses, through the Press, 30,000,000 people, who are impressed by what he gays, because it is a result of con- tinuous observation and deep study clothed in rhetorical eloquence. Ix speaking of the evils of over-work he says : " Oh, what an altar ! What a sacrifice of body, mind, and soul ! The physical health of a great multitude is flung on this sacrificial altar. They cannot sleep, and they 'make that an excuse for using chloral, morphine, Szc." Is there not some safe means by which we can make life easier? "CAPITAL" CHAT.

Mr. Wilson returns to the charge against the Bank of England and its directors with such in- creased positiveness and, indeed, directness of accusation in this month's "Investors' Review," that it is difficult to see how the challenge he vir- tttally throws down to put his allegations to the test Can be ignored.

It is clear that the last word has not yet been Said in the controversy that has recently been Waged between Messrs. Burns and Ilyndman. An *tort will be made—and it is possible that it may succeed—to induce Mr. Burns to visit Burnley in *pposition to Mr. Llyndman's candidature. It may be that Mr. Burns will have enough to engage his at- tention without undertaking much a duty, but there la little doubt that if it comes to a square fight the member will prove more than equal to the occasion.

In view af the statements that have been made in many quarters of late respecting the date of the sMt dissolution of Parliament, it may be worth notiug that within the last few days the Liberal Candidates for metropolitan constituencies have been advised to be prepared for a general election taking place at an early period of next year. There Is the best reason to believe that this warning has 'hem given on information supplied from tie Liberal headquarters.

Instructions have, I hear, been given to Lord 'Groaner, to intimate to the Khedive that we will have no more of his offensive show of anti-British feeling. We have had five of these exhibitions,and are satisfied that there is something beside boyish petulance behind. Lord Cromer is to tell Abbas rasha that the next time we have to complainof his conduct deposition will assuredly follow. Lord Reeebery's directions on that poie are very precise, sad admit of only one reading.

Lord. Rosebery is net well. He has been over- working himself, and at present he is suffering from neuralgic or rheumatic pains; and I believe the death of Sir Gerald Portal, whom he highly es- teemed, has greatly shocked the Foreign Minister. Indeed, business at the Foreign Office has latterly been quite ovei powering, and the situation created lu Egypt by the young Khedive's idiotic remarks is uot calculated to lessen Lord Rosebery's difficulties.

I hear on excellent authority that the Admiralty have now almost completed the necessary arrange- ments for the adoption of an eight hours day in all department. under their control. It is fully expected that an announcement to this effect will be made in the Queen's Speech at the commence- bent of the session, and there is even a possibility that the reform will be extended so as to include all Government employees. On the whole it looks as. if the Government were determined to make *eat session a memorattle one from the Labour point of view.

Re was the friend of the workers in every town and district in which his lot was cast, and his dedica- tion of his volume of sermons and essays, "Village to "The farm labourers of England, in aincere sympathy with their struggles towards Citizenship through self-reliance and association," sufficiently bespeaks the earnestness of his prin- ciples. The Church of England would not be un- popular if many of its ministers were like Dean WOW!.

This is an age of big things. We are worshippers of hugeness. An exhibition is not deemed a com- plete success if it is not bigger than all other pre- vious exhibitions. Sir Edward Watkin would deem his work in North London utterly valueless if it were not bigger than the structure given to Paris by the not too honest Mons. Eiffel. In a brief time some enterprising Yankee will undoubtedly build a tower in which Sir Edward's monstrous building could be comfprtably housed. Away in San Fran- cisco they are preparing the biggest statute on re- cord as an attraction for their forthcoming show. The Liberator swindle was the biggest of its class in the annals of finance. And the next war will be by far the biggest in the history of the% wqrld. Our generation is bent on rivalling the races who built the walls of Babylon and the great walls of China, and bequeathed us the Colossus of Rhodes.

Mr. Bainbridge, comptroller aid private secre- tary to the Duke of Edinburgh, arrived at Clarence House on Wednesday from Coburg, where he left everybody in a state of rapid preparation for the marriage of Princess Victoria Melita to the Grand Duke of Hesse. The Princess is radiantly in love with her future husband, and there courtship is, for fervour, very likely that of Prince Henry of Battenberg and Princess Beatrice, who were a most loving couple. The wedding is to take place in the third week in April if this date suits the Queen. All our Royal Family are to be present. The brides- maids will be the same as at the wedding of her sister, Princess Marie of Roumania. Everything for the wedding is to be got at Coburg, but large orders are to be executed in England. There is a statement that the nuptial knot is to be tied at Darmstadt, where the bride will in future reside.

The union between co-operation and trade union- ism which Mr. Tom Mann and others are anxious to bring about is coming into visible shape upon the feminine side. The various branches of the Women's Guild of Co-operation are taking up the question seriously, and are studying the problem of female unionism in connection with the trades of their respective districts. The Battersea Guild is said to have succeeded in improving the conditions of the work and wages for the girls in a local fac- tory, and is now engaged in helping the workers in another factory who have been out oa strike. In Bristol a similar policy has been followed. The Guild, it must be remembered, is recruited from among the wives and daughters of tne co-operators, and thus represents one of the steadiest and most self-respecting sections of the working classes. It is a good sign that they have taken to studyipg social problems outside the circle ,of the co-opera- tive store.

The Society for the Propagation of he Gospel has receiv3d a most important letter from the Bishop of Mashonaland touching his relations with Loben- gula. The Bishop speaks in high terms of the Matabele King, who "has been most considerate in not allowing houses belonging to missionaries or traders to be touched, or any European who stayed in his country while the fighting was going on to be injured." The Bishop volunteered to go and see the King and try and arrange something that might bring peace to the people; but his offer was not encouraged, as it was thought he might be killed by the warriors round the King. The Bishop, how- ever, took a different view, although he adds in his letter the insignificant remark that he did not know that if he went he would be of any value to the Company," for I could only recommend Lobengula to accept terms that I consider as advantageous to him as to them." In regard to the responsibility for the war, the Bishop distinctly asserts that no letter written or speech made urging on a war with the Matabele has ever had any sympathy whatever from him. He hoped to the very last that war would be avoided. He repudiates, too, with some warmth any share in the sentiment that "the sword is a necessary factor in the Christianising of those sav- age nations," or that" the only road for the preach- ing of Christianity is cleared by destroying their power. . . . The more rapid reception' be adds "of Christianity may be the outcome of the war, but rapid reception is not always most solid. I can only trust that God will in His good providence overrate for good all that may be wrong."

LECTURE ON BEETHOVEN.

On Monday evening a lecture on Beethoven was given in the Wesleyan Schoolroom, by the Rev. Geo. Ingram, 8.A., of Southport. The lecture was one of a course of lectures which are being arranged by the Yeung People's Society of Christian Endeavour. There was a good attendance, and the chair was occu- pied by the Rev. J. Kirtlan. The meeting was opened by a hymn sung to the tune "Fulda," composed by Beethoven, after which the Chairman briefly introduced the Lecturer. The Lecturer said that some seventy or eighty years ago, one of the greatest of German poets said that the French had gained the empire of the land, the English that of the water, and the Germans that of the air. This was certainly true in the resins of music, as they were the confessed masters of minstrelsy. He was going to tell them that night of one of the greatest of German musicians. Beethoven was horn on Dec. 17, 1770, in the town of Bonn, on the Rhine. His father was intemperate and cruel, but his mother was gentle and tender, and won the lifelong respect of her son. When but a child, he was kept at the piano, morning, noon, and night, until it became an instrument of tor- ture, and he was sometimes brought out of bed at night to give enjoyment to some of the roystering friends of his father. But it was to this early practice that he owed his facility of execution in future years, and it inured him to toil. At the age of eleven years he could find his way through the fugues of John Sabastian Bach. He had already written a sonata. He became assistant court organist at twelve years of age, and soon after- wards was enabled to go to Vienna to pursue his studies under Hayden, and here he met Mozart,who told some of his friends to take notice,of that youth, for he would be heard of again. Beethoven was an early riser, summer and winter, and was never tired of outdoor walks along the streams of the country. The lesson of his life was tireless activity. In the realm of music he was almost the same as Shakespeare in the realm of poetry. In both there was the flexibility of expression, and the same fecundity of thought. He composed chiefly for instru- mental music. The Lecturer next went on to examine various pieces of Beethoven's,and afterwards defended him from the charge of being misanthropic. His loss of hearing was a great affliction ; but some of his greatest pieces were written while he was totally deaf. He died on March 26, It27,from an illness contracted owing to a long journey in the cold to look after his scrapeerace nephew. Although neglected at the time of his death, there were thousands of people present at his funeral to pay tribute to one of the greatest musicians of his time. —During the course of the lecture Miss Snelson, Conser- vatoire of Music, Sondershausen, Germany, gave illus- trations from Beethoven's works in an excellent manner. The following were the selections :—Waltz, "Le esir ; scherzo and trio,from sonata in A b mAjor ; waltz. The Drum " ; march from " Fidelie." At the close of the evening a hearty vote of thanks was passed to the Lec- turer and Miss Snelson for their services.

SANKEY SCHOOL BOARD.

The monthly meeting of the Sankey School Board was held on Tuesday afternoon in the Boardroom, the chair being taken by Major Ridgway. The other members present were the Rev. E. S. Jackssn, Messrs. W. P. Skelton (vice-chairman), Joseph Wright, Henry Bate; George Clarke (schoolmaster), and Samuel Sutton (clerk).—The Clerk reported that there was a balance in the hands of the Treasurer amounting to £222 10s. 9d. After certain accounts had been paid, however, there would only be a balance in hand of £7o.—The Clerk said the next business before the Board was the consideration of the coal tenders. He had sent to six different people, but had only received three replies, namely, from Messrs. Evans and Co., Limited, W. W. Westwell and Co,, and Halton and Co. The contract was for twelve months.—After some con- sideration it was decided, on the motion of Mr. Skelton, to r" '.lt the tender of Messrs. "LUCKY EARLESTEAWN I"

Lucky Earlesteawn! Land o' Goshen ! Home o' mony a good owd chum ! • r 4 Paradise o' Love an' Lotion, 4 r Aw weel under one big thumb ! Theau's no need fer thowt fer th' morrow, Theau'rt a thrivin', healthy plant— Nice, snug, Railway Pocket Borough, Wot the dule moor du: ta want? Yo're too weel off, fer that matter, Yo ne'er buy a pig i'th poke; Arn't yo getten' lots o' wetter— Takkin' it fro other folk ? Look at yon new, splendid buildin', Oae oth' teawaship's grandest boons; An' heaw nicely its bin filled in, Fit fer servants' pantaloons ! Happy Earlesteawn, wi' this treasure Built ith' best three-corneit style, Isn't it a heaven-sent pleasure, Gawpin' on that noble pile? True, its welly dubble't th' taxes, Which we didn't know at fost ; But he's only a foo ez axes Owt abeawt this triflia' cost! WIDNES LIBRARY COMMITTEE. -- DEFEAT OF PROPOSAL FOR SUNDAY OPENING.

On Wednesday afternoon the monthly meeting of the Widnes Library Committee was held at the Town Hall, Mr. H. Wade Deacon, C.C., presiding. At the conclusion of the ordinary business, Council- lor O'Donnell, in accordance with a previous notice of motion, moved that the Central Reading-room be opened on Sundays, but said the hours would be entirely in the hands of the Committee. He thought the opening of the library on a Sunday would be a boon not only to the working population of the town, but to others. If he had his own way be would have the library open from noon each Sunday as a sort of counter-attraction to the public-house. He had taken the trouble to com- municate with several libraries, and he found that at St. Helens, Newcastle, Battersea, Clapham and Leamington Sunday opening had been a success but at Birkenhead it had failed. In Widnes they had 30,000 inhabitants,and he believed that if they opened the library on Sundays it would prove a success. With regard to the cost which opening on Sundays would entail, it would be very small, as Miss Proctor, the librarian, had promised to give her services free, and the library staff were, he believed, also willing. Councillor O'Donnell then moved i that the reading room be opened from six to ten on Sunday evenings. Mr. J. H. Lee seconded the motion. On the question of the Sabbath, he held himself as strongly puritanical as any member of the committee, and tie was himself at one time as much opposed to Sunday opening as any of them. But after thoughtfully weighing the question up he had come to the conclusion that by opening the lib- rary on Sundays they would be conferring a boon upon the working-class population. If the library was needed on week days it was doubly needed on Sundays.—Dr. O'Keeffe supported the motion.—Mr. S. Robinson op- posed the motion, and said public-houses being open was not an argument in favour of the Library being opened on Sundays. The public-houses should be closed, and he saw no reason whatever for creating a second divergence from church going and chapel going, and the worship of God on a Sunday. They had already Sab- bath desecration enough.—Mr.D.J. Brookes also opposed the motion.—Councillor Shores moved, as an amend- ment, "That Sunday opening be given a trial for two months." They would then, he said, be able to tell whether it was appreciate(' by the people.—Mr. a. K. Muspratt seconded.—Councillor Owens said he strongly opposed Sunday opening. He was a Sabbatarian, and if the motion was carried at that committee he would divide the Council op the question. After further discussion, Councillor Shores altered his amendment to three months,and then Councillor O'Don- nell embodied it in his motion. A vote was taken, and the motion was lost by 6 votes to 5. Councillors O'Donnell and Shores, Dr. O'Keefe, and Messrs. S.K. Muspratt and J. H.Lee voted for ; and Councillors Owen and Wright, and Messrs. S. Robinson, D. J. Brookes, F. Neil, ,and W. Sadler against. The Chairman did net vote.

IM.PERIAL fARLIAMENT.

MONDAY. Ihe House of Lords took into sonsideratioß in Mon- y evening the reasons of the House of Commons for disagreeing with the Lords' amendments to the Em- ployers' Liability Bill. The Marquis of RIPON moved that the Nouse should not insist on the contracting-out amendment of the Earl of Dudley. The Marquis of Salisbury said tho Opposition objected to the Govern- ment proposal as advocates of the freedom of the in- dividual and of the freedom of contract. Lord Forrer said the result of prohibiting contracting out would be to drive every employer into insurance with outside insur- an -e companies. The Marquis of Ripon said that in the opinion of the Government the existing insurance funds would in the main continue after the bill was passed. On a division Lord Ripon's motion was defeated by 125 to 22. Some minor amendments having been agreed to, the Earl of Dudley moved that the House should insist upon its original amenument as amended. The Marquis of Ripon observed that he should be deceiving the House if he were to say that in his judgment the amendments which had been made would afford any satisfactory solu- tion of the matter. Lord Dudley's motion was carried with-lut a division. The Marquis of Ripon moved that the House should not insist upon the amendment relat- ing to seamen, and, Lord Cross not insisting on this amendment, the noble Marquis's motion was agreed to. The Duke of Devonshire having during the discussion of one of the amendments reproached the Government with not having endeavoured to suggest improvements whi-h would have rendered such amendments unneces- sary, the Marquis of Ripon said the principle of the Government's oppositict to the contrasting,eut amend- ment remained entirely unchanged.

THURSDAY.

The House of Lords reassembled on Thursday even- ing and went into Committee OR the Local Government (Parish Councils) Bill. On clause 1 an amendment moved by the Earl of Winchilsea and Nottingham, pro- viding that there should be a parish council in every parish with a population of 500 (instead of 200), was carried by 137 to 80. Lord BALFOUR of Burleigh moved on clause 2 an amendment that the parish meeting should consist only of such persons as were on the local government register. The EARL ef KIMBERLEY and the DuKg of DEVONSHIRE opposed the amendment.which was supported by the MARQUIS Of SALISBURY. A division— taken on a technical question—gave the Opposition 112 votes and the Government 89, and after the EARL of SELBORNE had remarked that the question could be con- sidered hereafter the actual amendment was by consent withdrawn. A new sub-section to clause 3, providing that the district councillors should be ex-dicio members of the parish council, was carried by 60 to 55. An amendment by the EARL of SELBORNE, directed against the use of schoolrooms for meetings to discuss questions relating to allotments and candidatures, was carried by 148 to 28. The House sat till midnight, and got through eight clauses of the bill.

A BRADFORD MIRACLE.

A REMARKABLE RECOVERY.

From the " Bradford Daily Argus."

A representative of the "Bradford Daily Argus "has been led to inquire into the remarkable reports which have been a topic of conversation in Bradford lately, respecting the miraculous cures said to have been effected there,in cases regarded as hopeless,hy a remedy called Dr. Williams' Pink rills. It was pointed out to him that ouch a case existed in the person of Mr. Robert Fitzsimmons, of 33 Nazely-st., Sisterhills, Brad- ford, (Yorks), and he accordingly paid a visit to that address. Mr. Fitzsimmons is a joiner by trade, a mem- ber of the Amalgamated Society of Joiners, fifty years of age, and was born in Beckett-st.. Leeds. The first question the reporter put to Mr. Fitzsimmons (and here it may be added his wife was present at the interview, and fully endorsed his statement) was, " I understand you have been taking Dr. Williams' Pink Pills ?" - *. Yes, I have, lam thankful to say, and perhaps I had better tell you my own story," he went on. " I was working in Lancashire in 1886, and for a long time had to give up my work because of my rheumatic pain. I applied several remedies, but all to no purpose. When I came to Bradford I was again very ill, and I went into the Bradford Infirmary." "What were you suffering from then ?" queried the reporter. "Rheumatism ; that is what the doctors told me. I suffered very great pain, and then they got ma a recom- mend for Rawdon Convalescent Home (founded by the late Sir H. W. Ripley). I stayed there a few weeks, and then I came home again. We were living at Lidget Green then, and to. tell you the candid truth, sir, I suffered such great agony and pain that I could not bear the clothes on my body, and I have get up at four o'clock in the morning, and have crawled and walked about the garden to ease my mind from the pain. I could not, however, walk two yards, owing to the pain and weakness of my body. One day when I was in great agony I said to my missis, Shall we try Dr. Williams' Pink Pills ? ' She agreed with me that there could be no harm, and so I went to Taylor's Drug Stores and bought a box. After taking a few more boxes, I began to find speedy relief, and in a few weeks I was well. In fact, las as well now as ever I was, and can tramp about as much as ever I did. I have walked full Y ten or eleven miles to-day," and the route he named fully convinced the reporter that he had done so.

"I want to know what your honest opinion is—do you honestly think Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have done you good and restored your health " I do, sir, most truthfully. Since I took them in Ju'y, I feel a different man altogether. If you believe me, four months ago, I could not walk from that corner to this fireplace (an ordinary kitchen distance). I began to take Dr. Williams, Pink Pills last July, and after about six boxes I began to feel the real effects. I was taking them when the sew - lug machine man recommended me the citrate of iron and the quinine. I took it, but I thought it would kill me ; and I took Dr. Williams' Pink Pills,and all I can say is, the Pills restored me to the position you see me in now—and nothing else. I have taken nothing else, and have no doctor. In July last I was in such a languid state that I could scarcely walk or work, to tell you the can did truth, although I made many an attempt to• When I got at seven o'clock in the morning to the job in Horton-road, I was completely wearied and tired out, and had no energy to do one thing or the other. Of course,as I belong to the Amalgamated Society of Joiners and Carpenters, I got sick pay ; but a fellow does not want to throw himself on the Society for 12s. a week when he can earn, well, 323. a week when there is any work to be got. My rheumatism:is gone, and every way I feel better since I took the Pills.

An exchange of a few courtesies, and the interview terminated. The manifest improvement in Mr. Fitz- simmons' bodily strength, and his vastly improved ap- pearance since he began to take Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, is confirmed by his friends, Fend by the manager of Tay- lor's Drug Stores, who says that the numerous cases in Bradford where Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have effected a cure where no other medicine would do so, as well as their usefulness as a general tonic, have created an un- precedented demand for this remedy. This is convincing proof that Dr. Williams' Pink Pill for Pale People should be recognised as one of the greatest modern medicines, and a perfect blood builder and nerve restorer, curing such diseases as rheumatism, sciatica, neuralgia, partial piralysis, locomotor, ataxy, St. Vitus' dance, nervous headache, nervous prostration, and the tired feeling resulting therefrom, diseases de- pending upon humours in the blood, such as scrofula, chronic erysipelas, &c. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills restore pale and sallow complexions to the glow of health, and are a specific for all the troubles peculiar to the female sex, while in the ease of men they effAct a radical cure in all cases arising from mental worry, overwork, or ex- cesses of whatever nature.

These Pills are manufactored by Dr. Williams Medicine Company,of 46, Holborn Viieluct, London, England (and of Brockville, Canada), and are sold by chemists every_ where (but only in boxes with pink wrapper bearing the full name, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People), at 2s. 9d. a box, or six boxes for 135. 91., or they may be had direct by post from the Company at the same price. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People are never sold in bulk, or by the dozen cr hundred,4, and any dealer who offers substitutes in this form is trying to defraud you, and should be avoided.

SPEECH BY MR. JOHN CROSFIELD, .J.P.

An excellent programme was arranged by the committee of the Bank Quay Soap Works Social evenings committee for Saturday nights at the Bank Quay Mission Schoolroom. There was a very large audience, and the chair was occupied by Mr. John Crosfiehl, J.P. The CHAIRMAN in opening the proceedings, said that be was very much pleased to take the chair that even- ing, as it was the first time he had had the opportunity of being present at one of those evenings of healthy re- creation, as he might call them. He thought those even- ings were calculated to de a great deal of good to work- ing-men and their wives and children. He took it that every one present was either a workingman or a work- ingman's wife, it child. He believed that the object his son and Mr. Willett had in view was to give healthy and pleasing recreation to the workingmen. He thought that everything of that sort which produced good feel- ing and harmony between masters and men was very good,because they had all to remember that labour with- out capital was no good, and capital without labour was useless—(hear, hear)—and, therefore, it was always de- sirable that labour and capital should work together in harmony. (Hear, hear.) They had seem during the last year, unfortunately, a frightful ocample, of the effect this had produced when labour and capital had been antagonistic. That frightful coal strike, which it was estimated had caused this country a loss of £40,000,000 of money, and besides that loss of money, nobody knew what suffering and misery was caused in many a work- ingman's household. They knew that in Warrington, innocent people, who had nothing to do with the strike, but in consequence of the increased price of coal owing to the strike, were thrown out of employment. The large iion works were closed, and there were thousands of workingmen in Warrington thrown out of work. They had to depend upon the relief committee fir their pint of soup, and their pound of bread, per day. That was a melancholy thing, and he hoped that they might none of them live to see a repetition of such a state of things (Hear, hear). He was going to say that unselfishness was the corner stone of Christianity, and any man or woman who-wished happiness must have some thought of those outside themselves. He thought that credit was due to his son and Mr. Willett for having used their en- deavours to give that recreation to the people who were around them. He would like to give them an illustra- sion of one of the grandest men, and the most unselfish in this generation or in the past. He meant Richard Cobden. (Applause.) He was a man who left a busi- ness which was worth £BOOO a year—left it on purpose that he might abolish the cruel and wicked Corn Laws. At that time, the people were starving for want of bread, while there was corn rotting in the warehouses in Liver- pool. Richard Cobden went to another great man and noble patriot—John Bright. (Applause.) He found him mourning the loss of his wife, and said to him "Come with me, and we will not rest until we have abolished the Corn Laws," and they worked hard for six years, and in the end, they obtained the abolition of the Corn Laws. The consequence had been that ever since then, every man, woman and child in England had had cheap bread, and thus were better off than they were before. (Rear, hear.) That, he the (chairman), called a noble instance of self-sacrifice and unselfishness. He was a very young man at the time. Cobden was reduced to poverty, owing to that work, and a testimonial was got up for him. He thc (chairman) felt such affection and admiration for that man, that he gave half of what he possessed tithe testimonial. (Applause.)Richard Cobden set an example to everyone to do something as far as lay in their power to help those outside themselves,who might not be in the same happy position as themselves. In the beautiful words of Charles Kingsley, he would repeat : "Do noble deeds, not dream them all day long. And so make life, death, and the vast forever, a grand ewe et

song." (Applause.) The chief feature of the programme, of course, were the pianoforte recitals by Mons. Rossetti, who showed his wonderful mastery over the piano, by his recitals of " Tarantelle," " Rhapsodies," (Liszt), and Mendels- sohn's " Wedding March," for the latter of which he was encored. Miss Picton gave two solos, "The children's home," and" The Kerry Dance," for the latter of whieb she was encored, and by special request, she sang " The better land." Mr. Sewell sang the two bass solos, " The Diver," and "Friar of Orders Grey," in good style. Mr. James Hayes sang the comic songs "The Grass Widower," " The future Mrs. Awkins," "The shop walker," and "My old Dutch," each song eliciting an encore. Mr. Greener recited is an excellent manner, "One niche the highest," and "Nocturnal sketch" (Tom Hood), for the latter of which he was encored, and gave the hunting scene in Pickwick. Mr. Wiwerr in moving a vote of thanks to the Chair- man for presiding over the concert, said they were very much indebted to him,also to Mr. Arthur and Mr. John. They were doing all they could to cultivate a good feel- ing between masters and men. Be also proposed a vote of thanks to the ladies and gentlemen who had assisted in the entertainment. He thanked Mr. Crosfield for the remarks about himself, but there was a large committee which did a lot of work for the entertaioment.—The motion was passed unanimously and the meeting closed.

PREPARING FOR THE FIRST MEETING.

AS. Helens correspondent was on Wednesday in- formed by a prominent member of the Miners' Concili- ation Board, which meets on the 13th inst., that every- thing is now practically settled as between the two sections, except the vital questions of a minimum wage and the price of coal. THREATENED GREAT RAILWAY sT EKE IN IRELAND.

Tuesday next has been fixed as the date upon which nearly the whole of the Irish Great Northern Railway, employees will go on strike if the directors in the mean- time do not acquionce in their demands. The disaffected railway men include stationmasters, booking clerks, platelayers, signalmen, guards, and porters. They ask for 30 per cent. increase and a reduction of hours. The men have during the past few mouths held a consider- able number of private meetings all over the systena, the organisation being now almost perfect.
