HARVEY'S OYSTER COMPANY, L EMITED.

A STORY OF COMPANY FLOATING.

At the London Bankruptcy Court, on Monday, the public examination of the officials into the formation and working of this Company was held before Mr. Re- gistrar Meller. The company was formed in April,lB93, to acquire two businesses with branches carried on by Mr. Harvey, of Dudley, and Mr. Fearon, of Liverpool, with the various branches at Birmingham,Cheltenham,Derby, Douglas (Isle of M4n), Walsall, Wolverhampton, and Dublin, with oyster beds at Bangor, for which the sum of £lB,OOO was to be paid, £12,000 to Mr. Harvey, and £6,000 to Mr. Fearon. The Company went to allotment upon a subscription of £10,200, of which £lO,OOO was provided by underwriters who afterwards failed to meet their engagements. The first witness was Mr. F. A. Flood, the promoter, who was mainly questioned as to the circumstances under which the underwriting of the shares was obtained. He stated that he was introduced to a Mr. Wilkinson, who he was informed was the managing director of the British Union Association Company, who had floated some 40 companies success- fully. He was Surprised afterwards to see that the European Financial and Investment Corporation's name was introduced in the contracts. It was not until after the company went out to allotment. He had no idea that such an arrangement was entered into, and he had no idea previously that such a company existed. He promised Mr. Long £5OO for his introduction, and Mr. Wilkinson £l5OO, he (Mr. Wilkinson) having stated that he should require £5O each for the signatories to the underwriting contracts. A question was raised as to the right of counsel represent- ing Mr. Wilkinson to cross-examine the witness, when it was arranged that after Mr. Wilkinson was examined, the witness should be re-called, and questioned by Mr. M•Kasker,the learned counsel in question.—Jas. Harvey examined by Mr. Stewart, stated that he arranged to pay Mr. Haywood £3,000 to form his business into a company. This was not carried out, and he afterwards saw Mr. Flood, the previous witness. He was to get £12,000 and Mr. Fearon £6,000. He had made as much as £2,000 a year out of his business, but had never kept any books. He had always made a good living out of it. In 1891 he should think he made £lOO a year clear profit. In 1892 he made no profit at all, owing to the oysters being killed by the frost.—By the Registrar: His profits the last five years hafl been about £2OO a year.—By Mr. Stewart: He gave certain figures to the accountant who prepared the report in the prospectus. —Mr. Stewart: And brought out upon your figures, at a profit of over £2,500, and yet you have just said that you made no profit in 1892-93. How did you arrive at that ?—Well,l considered the profit I should make with the assistance of the extra capital and the wholesale business that was to be done. He had had a correspon- dence with the promoter. Mr. Flood, to the effect that the valuation in the accountant's report was too much: however, the figures were not altered.—Questioned as to the wnolesale business referred to in the prospectus, were those of Mr. Fearon, at New Bright.-n and Warrington, which were to be acquired by the company. Mr. Stewart : And which the company did not acquire ? Because they could not pay for it. Continuing,the wit- ness said that he thought the statement in the prospec- tem as to rapidly increasing business in oysters had reference to the oyster trade generally and not be his own business. (Laughter.) He admitted that he was to be paid practically £ll,OOO for the goodwill of his busires.—Questioned as to the underwriting of the shares, he was positive that he was told that Mr. Wilk- inson was the managing director of the British Union Assurance Company, that was going to underwrite the shares. Mr. Long introduced him to Mr. Wilkinson and borrowed a" quid or two " off him by way of commis- sion for the introduction he supposed. (Laughter). Mr. Wilkinson told him that his company had successfully floated 40 companies, and were very particular as to the statement in the prospectus. He never heard of the European Investment and Financial Company until afterwards, when the underwriting contracts came to be looked into,when it was fsund that there were other names on the contracts. In reply to Mr. Noton,witness stated that all the information given as to the takings at the various branches Was obtained from the manager at each branch.—James Herbert Wilkinson was then called, and stated that he was managing director of the European Investmentand Finance Company and the Bri- tish Union Assurance Corporation. The latter corporation did not finance companies ; the former corporation were agents for the procuration of under writers. He was a subscriber to European, &c., Company, holding one share, he being described as of Norfolk-street, Manches- ter, accountant, his wife described as an artist of South- port, held one share. Mr. Ernest Long, of Birmingham, and Mrs. Long, of Liverpool, held a share each. No calls had been made in that company, no capital having been required. The various gentlemen who signed the underwriting contracts were called, all of them stating that they understood that the contract carried no liabil- ity whatever, and that they received £5O for so signing. Major-General Sir John Campbell, Bart., C. 8., a director, said that he had taken particular care in investigating the statements in the prospectus as far as he could. That there were no books of Harvey's was true, and he did the best he could under the circumstances. He thought it a very good company, but invested no money in it. Mr. W. Pering Paige was another dlrecter, and stated that he had visited the shops at Cheltenham, Bir- mingham, and Dudley. He invested the money in the company, and it was upen his petition that the winding up order was made. J. E. Thompson, another director, thanked God that this was the only company he was a director of. But for believing that the British Union Assurance Company were underwriting the £lO,OOO he should not have consented to go to allotment. The ex- amination was adjourned generally.

COLLIERY DISPUTES IN SOUTH LANCASHIRE.

THREATENED STOPPAGES.

At several of the South-west Lancashire collieries dis- putes have arisen which threaten to terminate in another struggle between capital and labour. A press representative waited upon Mr.S.Woods, M.P.,president of the Lancashire Miners' Federation, and upon Mr. T. Glover, J.()., agent for St. Helens, on Thursday morn- ing, and was informed that at the Edge Green Colliery of Messrs. Richard Evans end Co. a dispute had arisen. Mr. Woods and a deputation of twelve or fourteen men waited upon Mr. John Robinson, the manager, and stated their objection to lower prices being fixed. A reply was promised, but had not yet been received, and fourteen days notice to stop work, to expire on the 13th inst., had been given. Only a small number of men were affected, but they were resisting a principle which might spread throughout the great coalfield worked by Messrs. Richard Evans and Co. At Eccleston Hall Colliery. St. Helens, the men ' were very dissatisfied with their wages, contending that ' the field prices were fixed too low. Mr. Woods had been in correspondenee with Mr. Walker, the manager for the Worsley Mesnes Colliery Company, and there was a hope that the grievance would be remedied. At the Lea Green Colliery, St. Helens, it had been reported that an attempt had Peen made to interfere with prices' and Mr. Woods had pointed this out to Mr. Campbell the manager, and had stated that it might lead to further tronble4 as the miners would not suffer any tampering with field prices. A reply had been received,and the matter was under inves- tigation. A dispute affecting 201) men had alse arisen at the Park Lane Collieries, near Brynn. The men had had certain prices for four or five years, but a short time since the manager pi oposed a lower rate. They had had 2s. a ton, in- clusive of removing dirt, but the alteration proposed wahp.s. qd. per ton for coal and extra for dirt. The reduction thus proposed would mean 2d. or 3d. per ton, which the men would not accept. A deputation had just waited upon the manager, and it was arranged that Mr. Greene'', one of the federation officials, should go , and measure the thickness of the dirt, and Mr. Woods should check the wages. The dispute involved a serious principle, but he (Mr. Woods) hoped it would be satis- factorily settled. The federation had considered the matter, and had agreed to support the men. Mr. T. Glover, J.P., states that he has been in communica- tion with Messrs. Glover, Urmson and Glover, of the Phcenix Colliery, St. Helens, regarding the ' payment of wages. At present the firm pay on Saturdays, and a request has been made for payment on Fridays. The firm state that Friday is incon- venient for them, but the men insist upon their request being complied with. A serious dispute had arisen at Brynn Hall Colliery, one of the first to resume work at the old rate of wages during the recent strike. He (Mr. Glover) and Mr. Woods had been in communi- cation with the proprietors. It arose on the fixing of field prices in the Arley Mine. About 1,000 employees were affected. The manager had been seen several times, but up to Thursday morning Mr. Glover had not heard of any settlement. The Brynn Hall Collieries were not represeuted by an agent, but the Lancashire and Ches- hire Miners' Foderation had taken up the matter. The matter is not yet settled. ' As regards the rumour that Mr. S. Woods would con- test St. Helens in the Labour interest if his namesake (Mr. W. Woods) declined, Mr. S. Woods says he has no intention of severing his connection with Ince, where the best feeling prevails. MR. PICKARD AND THE CONCILIATION BOARD. At Barnsley,on Thurslay, Mr. Pickard, M. P. President of the Miners' Federation of Great Baitain,alludine to the statement that all the work connected with the Concilia- tion Board was agreed to, with the exception of the minimum wage and the controlling of selling prices said this was news to him. If everything was settled, ;here aas little to do. He hoped such was the case, but information from those who attended the meeting was to the effect that nothing was settled.

Mr. Arnold Morley has accomplished some Weful work in connection with the Post Office S ivings Bank. Dur- ing 1890 no less than 268,000 hours overtime was workal in the office. Now the overtime in the department is practically confined to one month in the year—in other words it has been reduced to inconsiderable limits. The Savings Bank staff twelve months as,o entered an empha- tic and explicit protest against extra duty, and Mr. Morley's poliey has been to satisfy their protest.

ST. HELENS.

SUDDEN DEATH OF A COLLIER.

John Frocisham, a collier,living at Haresfinch Cottages S. Helens. was found by his wife in a dying condition on Wednesday, and before assistance could be rendered, he was dead.

It is believed that there are several seams of good coal in the neighbourhood, and that they will be touched when operations are resumed.

ROBBING A M.

Julia Martin and Fa-t-li-e;:irie. Carney, two well known charact-rs, were on Thursday, at St. Helens, charged with stealing two half sovereigns from the person of Thomas Orby. He was met by the women on Wednes- day night, and they asked for a drink. He took them into the Star Hotel, where they saw him handle the half sovereigns and some silver. They afterwards robbed him.—The prisoners were committed for two months with hard labour.

CATHOLIC CHARITY BALL.

At the Town Hall, on Wednesday night, the fifty- seventh annual ball in aid of the Catholic charities was held, and was successful both as regarded attendance and general arrangements. About £BO is annually raised by the event, which this year was under the patronage of the Mayor (Mr. A. Sinclair), the Marquis of Ripon, the Earl of Derby, Viscount Cross, G.C.B.,Lord Arundel' of Wardour, Lord Gerard of Brynn, and many other gentlemen, Lady Gerard was patroness, Messrs. W. H. Chisnall, G. Stringfellow, and A. A. Slater were the M.C.'s, and Mr. E. Clayton's band occupied the or- chestra.

BREAKING A MIN'S JAW.

On Tuesday, Thos. Mawdslev,a well known character. of 145, Park-road,who did not appear,was charged with assaulting Thomas Connor. The complainant, who ap- peared with his head bandaged,said that on the 6th inst. he was in the Half-mile beerhouse, and Mawdsley asked him to pay for a pint of beer. He refused, and defend- ant struck him several times on the faoe, breaking his jaw. Ho was unable to work, and was attended by a doctor.—Chief Constable Wood said Mawdsley had been convicted many times before. Mawdsley was fined £2 and costs, or a month's imprisonment.

LABOUR INTERESTS.

The newly-formed Independent Labour party for West Sutton, St. Helens, has just adopted the following as the fundamental principles of the branch :—" That the programme of the party be the nationalisation of the land and other instruments of production ; that the party shall devote itself to securing the election of members to all representative bodies for the purpose of realising the programme of the party ; and that all members of this party pledge themselves to abstain from voting for any other candidate for election where an Independent Labour candidate is contesting."

DEATH OF 1141 OLD TRADESMAN. The death took place on Sunday of Mr.e. Mearns, aged sixty-nine years, father of Councillor J. P. Meanas, solicitor, St. Helens. Mr. ?deems was a native of Bel- fast, but came to St. Helens forty years ago. He carried on an extensive business as a provision dealer for many years, but of late had been practically free from trade matters. He was associated with one of the oldest Roman Catholic families in the neighbourhood. Although a sound Glacistonian Liberal, he took no active part in politics, chiefly devoting his time to pedestrian exercise and reading.

TEE GAMBLE INSTITUTB.

In preparing the amended and practically new plans for the Gamble Institute, St. Helens, the architects. Messrs. Briggs and Wolstenholme, have had a pleasing feature to consider. The donor, Colonel Gamble, has purchased some house property adjoiaing the site, and thus light is secured to the rear ot the building. This has enabled the main building to be reduced one storey, and the filling up of the'spac,e which had been reserved for light. I splendid building of handsome proportions to cost within the £20,000 donation is expected.

MEETING OF WATCHIsi&KIIRS AT PRESCOT.

A fairly well attended mseting 4 the Liverpool alxl District Watchmakers' and Jewellers' Association was held in the Town Hall, Prescit, on Monday night. Mr. Thomas H. Chesterton, of St. Helens, presiding, to con- sider the proposal to establish a branch of the above as- sociation at Pre.,cot. Mr. W. A. Newcombe, organising secretary, explained the object of the association, and a resolution in favour of forming a branch of the Liver- pool and District Watchmakers' and Jewellers' Associa- tion was carried, and steps were at once taken to carry out the project.

_ _ Tan TRADE DEPRESSION. The directors of the St. Helens Cafe and Recreation Company, Limited, in their annual report just issued regret that the business of the company duriag the past year has been very injuriously affected by the general depression in trade, and particularly by the disputas in the glass bottle and coal trades. There had been a con- siderable decrease in the buMness done at both of tbe company's cafés. The profit and loss account showed a loss on the year's working of £55 10s. 81, which,after deducting the sum of £5 9s. sd. brought from the pre- vious year, left a net adverse balance of £5O is. 31 The new eeNtral café had not answered the expectations of the directors. The retiring directors this year are Messrs. C. Sharpies, T. Cook, and J. Harrison, all of whom are eligible for re-election.

LICENSING APPLICATIONS AT ST. HELENS.

At St. Helens Police Court, on Thursday, Mr. John Seddon, captain of the St. Helens Recs. Football Club, applieri to the magistrates (Aid. J. Harrison and Mr. H. Martin) for a temporary authority to sell at the Hope and Anchor Inn, Windle City, until the next transfer day. Mr. Thomas, for the applicant, stated that Seddon was at present the teliant of the Young Prince beerhouse Westfield-street, but the purchase of the Hope and Anchor at Windle had been effected under the will of the late owner and the tenancy hithertc, now expired. Seddon was anxious to remove therein at orce. and accordingly made the present application. Chief-con- stable Wood said he had no objection, and the magis- trates granted the application. The magistrates also granted a temporary anth.rity to John Hewitt to sell at the Young Prince, Westfield-street, until the next trans- fer day.

DROWNING CASE

On Monday afternoon the body of John Cordon, aged 25 years, a boilermaker of Double Locks, Parr, St. Helens, was taken out of the St. Helens and Sankey Canal about 100 yards from his home. Cordon left home in his usual health about six o'clock the previous night to visit some friends. At half-past nine he was with his brother-in-law in the Albion Hotel, and was then quite- sober. He shortly afterwards called at a house inTark- road, some little distance from his home, and was Own noticed to be slightly under the influence of drink. When toe body was taken from the canal by relatives,who had been dragging it, it was found that his watch bad stopped at 9.50, and that was the time he was supposed to have fallen into the water, after leaving Park-road. pile had been teetotal since Christmas, but had broken the pledge on Sunday. He leaves a widow and one child.

THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 3. 1894.

"WHY the Bill was nothing but A Chaltenge to Trades Unionism. a Trades Union Bill." Such is the remark, says a Parliamentary correspondent, wbich.a member of the House of Lords passed upon the Employers Liability Bill as their lordships defiled away to their homes on Monday afternoon after practically destroying the Bill. We say " destroyed" because by insist- ing upon their contracting out amendment the Peers have so mutilated the Bill that it will not be carried forward by the Government, and it will be rejected with scorn by the Trades Unionists of the country. The Lords have, as a matter of fact, at the instigation of Lord SALISBURY and the Tory party in general, declared war upon Trades Unionism. The Employers Liability Bill was, in a pre-eminent degree, a Trades Union measure. The hereditary legislator who made the remark we have quoted at the beginning of this article was right in that respect. Because the Bill con- tained provisions which are urgently demanded by the combined workmen of the country, and by many who are not yet enrolled in any trade organi- zation, a Liberal Government has given it a foremost place in its programme, and has pressed It forward through the House of Commons despite the threats and the cajolery of all the forces of reaction. For the same reason, that is to say, because it is a measure strongly desired by the organised workmen of the country, the House of Lords has resolved to ruin the Bill by eliminating from it its most vital prin- ciple. There can be no mistake upon this point. Their lordships knew perfectly well before Mon- day that adherence to their contracting out proposals would involve the abandonment of the Bill, and in the debate on Monday Lord RIPON again warned them to the same effect in plain and unmistakeable language. Nor is there any room for mistake as to the attitude that the Peers have taken up in regard to Trades Unionism. The Government, said Lord SALISBURY, were in the matter of that Bill the " victims of dire necessity and the slaves of a cruel organisation." "A cruel organisation " is Lord SALISBURY'S description of the combined forces of Trades Unionism, and, be it remembered, Lord SALISBURY is the leader of the Tories not only in the ouse of Lords but throughout the whole of the country. This latest manifestation of the contempt and the llitter hostility with which they are regarded by the leader of their own party, must surely be the most nauseous pill which the Tory workingman and trades unionist has had to swallow for a long time back. The House ef Lords is an exceedingly undemonstrative assembly. A speaker is there rarely encouraged by even a ripple of applause. But, we are told by one correspondent, when the Marquis of SALISBURY alluded to the million and a half of English trades unionists in muchthe same fashion as a Continental States- man might refer to Anarchists or Nihilists the Peers" enjoyed their relaxation with almost inde- cent glee." There can now, in fact, be no manner of doubt that the virtual rejection of the Em- ployers Liability Bill is a defiance and a challenge to the trade unionists of the sountry. There is, we believe, just as little doubt that this gage of battle, so wantonly thrown down, will be taken up with a readiness and a vigour that will sur- prise those Peers who are taking it upon them- selves to mar all progressive legislation.

THERE can be no finer tribute to The esigna Rumoured the amazing force and influence . Rtion. which Mr. GLADSTONE continues to display, in spite of his more than four score years, than the impatient de sire which Is mani- fested in some quarters to hear "of his " resigna- tion " or of his "retirement from political life." The PREMIER is at the present time enjoying a well-earned holiday at Biarritz, and the opportun- ity has been seized by a London evening paper which, perhaps, stands much in need of a bold advertisement, to once more announce his "impending resignation." The "Pall Mall Gazette," with solemn assumption of superior knowledge, stated on Wednesday that it had reason to know from an authority which it was not able to disclose, but in which it "had every confidence" that Mr. GLA.DSTONE had finally decided to resign office almost immelately ; and, further, that the letter announcing his resolution would be sent to the Queen before the assembling of Parliament. Then, condescending to particu- lars, it was said that the reasons for this very important decision were—first, a sense of advanced age and the great strain of the late arduous session; second, disappointment at the rejection of the Home Rule Bill and at the oppo,3i- tion to the Parish Councils Bill; and, finally, domestic pressure. This announcement which must have given infinit e satisfaction to the sensa- tional journalist who concoQted it had the run of London and the country for several hours. Mr ULADSTONE was, however, communicated with by telegraph, and the first sentence of the reply con- tained a complete denial. It was that" the state- ment that Mr. GLADSTONE has definitely decided, or has decided at all, upon resigning office is un- true." The message continued as follows : 'lt is trua that for many months past his age and the condition of his sight and hearing have in his judgment made relief from public cares desirable, and that, accordingly, his tenure of office has been at any moment liable to interruption from these causes in their nature permanent. It remains ex- actly as it has been.. He is ignorant of the course which events important to the nation .may take ev en (luring the remainder of the present s.eaton, and he has not done or said anything which could in any degree restrain his absolute freedom and that of his colleagues with regard to the perform- ance of arduous duties now lying or likely to lie be- fore them."

Some ardent admirers and supporters of Mr. PLADSTONE are disquioted by the latter part of his message. It is not SP emphatic in its denial of the possibility of resignation as they could have wished. In reality, however, it does not indicate that Mr. GLADSTONE has now the question of resignation eny more under considera- tion than at any time during the past two or three years. He has often reminded his followers that his future political work is entirely depeudent

on the measure of health and strength which he • may continue to enjoy, and his latest message goes no further than this. During the recent sit— ting of Parliament he surprised friend and foe alike by his physical and mental vigour, and there is every reason to believe that he will continue to lead the Liberal party through the remaining period of the present Parliament, and at the next? general election.

Weighty Words THE "British Medical Review" of a publishes at a very opportune Wise Physician. time some notes of a conversation with the late Sir Andrew CLARK on Mr. GLAD- STONE'S health. The notes were taken last year when the periodical rumour was floated as to the retirement from public life of the veteran Premier, and they will help to silence the "chatter of ir- responsible frivolity" that we have heard this , week. "It is quite untrue," said Sir ANDREW, "that I have urged Mr. GLADSTONE to withdraw from public work, and quite unlikely that I ever should do so. In the first place, the responsibi- lity would be far beyond what any physician would be justified in assuming, unless in the case of an imminent break down, and which Mr. GLADSTONE. shows no sign whatever; and, in the second place, Mr. GLADSTONE is a man of marvellous physical as well as mental endowments; and, notwithstand- ing lais advanced years, he has in many respects still all the elasticity and vigour of youth. His muscular and arterial systems are both extra- ordinarily well preserved, and the force, energy, and versatility of his nervous powers are far be- yond those of an ordinary man in the prime of life. Above all, he has the inestimable power, acquired partly by long habit and partly the re- sult of admirable balance of the nervous system, of sleeping long and well throughout the night, however agitating the events of the day may have been." We are now told that chagrin at the rejection of the Home Rule Bill is one of the reasons which have brought about the .mythi- cal resignation. HD!, little does this " reason " harmonise with the testimony of 'Sir ANDREW CLARK. "Even after the rejection of the Home Rule Bill, and the crisis which followed," said the great physician, "Mr. GLADSTONE'S sleep was unimpaired and his physical condition uninjured." Then we are told that to "Mr. GLADSTONE, work

is not exhausting, but restorative; it is his true stimulus, and keeps him young and vigorous. I would," continues Sir ANDREW CLARK, "never advise him to relapse into in- activity, even to prolong his life. Ido not think it would have such an effect, but rather the opposite." To those who would fain.believe that Mr. GLADSTON El is on the eve of an immediate re- tirement, we would commend a considoratio n

the passages we have just quoted.

Special temperance sermons were preached in several Warrington and district churchos on Sunday, in response to the request of the Lord Bishop of Liverpool,and collections taken in aid of the Church of England Temperance Society. The services were generally well attended.

SRI:LIMN ALLEGATIOXS AGAINST WIDNES DOCTORS. During the hearing of a case under the Elementary Education Act at Widnes, on Thursday, the health of a child of one of the defendants was an important con- sideration. A certificate from a medical gentleman in the town was produced, but the attendance officer (Mr. Grant), who supported the case, said that certificates of that kind were granted by doctors who never saw the patients.—The Chairman : I take it you do not mean your statement to apply to this particular instance, but to cases generally ?—Witriess : Yes.—The Chairman : Your statement is a serious one, and I advise you to be more cautious in future. CLUB. The third of a series of billiard handicaps was brought to a conclusion at the Volunteer Social Club thin week. The first handicap, a cue presented by Mr. Arthur Tim- mins, was won by that gentleman, who was placed 110 points behind scratch. The second handicap, confined to volunteers, was won by Corporal Neild, the cue and case in this instance being presented by Mr. George ilhone, of the George and Dragon, who is a retired ser- geant. Corporal Neild was also successful in running the third cue, which was presented by Messrs. Orme, of Manchester. A fourth handicap has just been com- menced for a guinea cue, presented by Mr. Torn Jones, and a case presented by Mr. Timmins.

RUNCORN GAS COMPANY. The half-yearly meeting was held on Thursday morn- ing, Mr. Fred Rigby, J.P., chairman of directors, pre- sided, and there were present, Major Hazlehurst, J.P., Messrs. James Handley, J.P., W. Whittaker, Thomas Handley, James Pritchard, J. M. Frith, G. S. Frith, and J. R.Frith (secretary). The directors' report stated that the prolonged coal strike involving an additional outlay of £450 for fuel interfered greatly with the business of the Company,both as regards coal and cannel supply and the consumption of gas. The revenue account showed a profit of £3,861 18s. for the year. In order to pay the interest on debentures and declare a dividend of ten per cent. which- the directors recomtnended, a further sum of £432 ss. sd. would be required. This it was proposed to charge against the balance, standing to profit and loss account, leaving a credit balance of £730 53. 3d. to that account. The reserve fund invested in consols,now stdod at £3,533 6s. 2d. The new works are ready for the manufacture of gas and will shortly be brought into use.—The Chairman moved and Mr. James Handley seconded the motion. It was remarked that considering the crisis through which the company had passed, the payment of the usual dividend without increasing the price of gas to consumers, was satisfactory both to shareholders and the general public.—The report was adopted. ALLEGED THEFT OF kONEY.

At the Warrington Occasional Court on Thursday before Mr. James Tinsley, James Hatton was charged with stealing 7s. 6d. in money, from the drawer in the office of Peter Wood, Padgate on Jan. 31. Peter Wood said that he was a coal dealer in Padgate, and on Jan. 31st, he left some money in his drawer. At about five o'clock in the afternoon, the defendant came into the office and stood awhile warming himself by the fire during which time complainant went out for about ten minutes. When he got back, the defendant said that it was time to be going and went out. Shortly afterwards, com- plainant had occasion to open the drawer, and found a part of it disarranged. On examining it, Me discovered that 75.6 d, had gone. The police were informed and P.C. Head arrested the defend- ant who denied having taken the money. He was remanded to the Warrington County Petty Sessions.

Linaker, manager of the Frodsham Town Hall, and Mr. ; Henry Hasslsg,rave, manager of the " Grelley's Money Company," now being produced at Blackpool, were res- pectively summoned for keeping a hall wherein stage plays were prodnee.i, and for presenting " Grelley's Money," "Hamlet." and "East Lynne," when the place was not licensed. AN EFFECT OF THE GALE.—OR Saturday afternoon the high wind blew down a large tree by Mr. Matthew Pownall's farm. The tree fell across the road and the traffic was impeded for some time. One of Mr. Maginn's horses while passing stumbled and fell, but happily sus- tained no serious injury. The tree was with great trouble rolled on the footpath but as it was considered dangerous to pe4estrians, it was moved to the other side. This was done with great difficulty and the busses and several carts were delayed for some time. THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN.

BY AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT.

Mr. James Handley, J.P., has been elected presi- dent of Garston Liberal Club.

Everybody is practically agreed that eight hours is quite long enough to labour at some of the occupations of a chemical works, and it is a cause of satisfaction that the United Alkali Co. are estab- lishing the system in their Runcorn, Widnes and Weston works. The company deserve credit for se promptly adopting the wtshes of the Royal Com- mission.

The preparations for the annual treat to the aged persons of Runcorn are nearly completed, and the event will take place in the Public Hall on Tuesday evening next. The old folks will be treated with the generous hospitality of former years, and a suitable entertainment will be provided after the feast. Over 500 guests are expected.

Another victim to the death-trap of the Weston Canal. Mr. Wm. Statham, was drowned there be- tween Tuesday night and Wednesday morning. The place is absolutely dangerous, the rails over the Bridge leading down to the water's edge, and it is said they are placed that way for the convenience of the boatmen and their horses. The road is a private one, and it is surprising that so many people fre- quent it when there is a m mill safer awl equally near way to Runcorn through the Saltworks.

THE EX. MINE • SA T URDAY, FEBRUARY 3. 1894.

THE RUNCORN DEATH TRAP.

SAD END OF MR. WILLIAM STATHAM.

What Mr. Coroner Worsley has frequently describ- ed as Runcorn's death-trap, had another victim on Tuesday, in the person of Mr. William Statham, builder and contractor of Trentham-st., Runcorn. Mr. Statham who is building some cottages at Wes- ton Point, spent the day at that place and in the even- ing visited the Waterman's Arms, kept by Mrs. Fitton. He left there at 10 p.m. and next morning his body was found floating in the Weston Canal. He had as umbrella, under his arm and wore a macintosh which it is con- sidered caused the body to float. It is believed that he accidentally walked into the canal. The deceased who was born at Stathain, pear Lymm, came to Runcorn about 33 years ago as sub-contractor for the brickwork at Weston Chemical works. Some years later he com- menced business on his own account as a contractor and builder, and had a fairly successful career. He was widely known and respected, and the greatest sympathy is felt for his only daughter in her deep sorrow. The inquest was held yesterday, by Mr. J. E. WorsleY, ' Mr. George Hewitt, ship chandler, Percival-lane, said he was no relation to the deceased but had known him 18 years. He resided with the deceased who was a builder. He was 59 years of age. He last saw him alive on Tues- day morning. He was in good health. On Tuesday Right, it was very dark and wet. The deceased said he had some business to transact at Weston and Weston Point. It had been his custom to walk along the canal, and as late as he would do on the night in question.— Mrs. derail Fitton, landlady of the Waterman s Arms, at Weston Point, deposed that the deceased had been in the habit of coming to her house for seven years, as he had nearly always been building there. On Tuesday night he left her house at ten o'clock and she spoke to him as he went out. He was in excellent health and spirits. He was quite sober when he left. If fact, he had not been taking much to drink since Christmas. The night was very dark and windy and wet. Her nephew asked if he should accompany him, in fact he always did because it was a dangerous road. The deceased declined the offer and said he could manage. —ln reply to Mr. Jolliffe, who appeared on behalf of the relatives,the witness said the deceased had business with her on Tuesday night.—John Jeff, banksman on the Weston Canal, said he found the body of the deceased in the Canal on Wednesday morning. The body was floating in the lower section of the Canal. P.O.Benfield said he had examined the body. The clothes were all in order. In the pockets were a silver watch and chain aed ss. 6d. There were no marks ot viol- ence on the body.—The Coroner characterised the case as a very sad one and said there could be little doubt that he fell into the canalswhile walking home on a dark and stormy night. This was another of those cases that had come into his court from time to time, and which so long as that road from Weston Point to Runcorn re- mained in the position that it was, would from time to time come there. The numiser of valuable lives sacrificed by people coming along that dangerous road on dark nights and stormy weather, I erf ,ctly sober they might be, he said nothing of those cases in which the people had been drunk,was appalling. He had not the numbers before him, but he should think there were over 20 eases where the facts were the same as brought before them on this occasion, He did hope when the: arrangements of the Ship Canal were perfected, something would be done to close that road against the public, either by the substitution of a better road or by rendering this one safe so that this lamentable loss of life might not occur. He was sure he was expressing the feelings of the jury when he said they sympathised with the friends and relatives of Mr. Statham for the loss they had 'sustained by his death under these unfortunate circumstances.— Mr. Williams (the foreman) asked the Coroner if he would accept a verdict of accidentally drowned instead of found drowned. These eases had occurred so often and no steps had been taken to make the road safe. Found drowned suggested nothing whereas a verdict of accidentally drowned, might lead the authorities to take some steps.—The Coroner: I feel certain it is an acci- dent; at the same time found drowned is your correct verdict.—Mr. Williams : The unanimous wish of the jury is accidently drowned.—The Coroner: Very good.—A verdict of accidentally drowned was recorded.—The funeral took place at Runcorn Cemetery yesterday after- noon in the presence of a large concourse of people. The body, enclosed in an oak coffin with brass furniture was carried to its last resting place in the handsome hearse of Messrs. Yarwood Bros., and in the coaches which fol- lowed were the following mourners: Messrs. Richard Statham (brother), Jno. Davies (brother-in-law), Gwilt Catterall, (Wrexham), Jno. Davies (Montgomeryshire), George Hewitt, Thomas Weir, William Hewitt, and William Turner.

BICKLEY-STREET MISSION BA.ND.

In connection with Buckley-st. Mission, Warr- ington, there is a band which, during the past two years, has given considerable promise of becoming one of our leadinglocal bodies of brass instrumenta- lists. There is one matter in connection with the Land, however, that has so far had a detrimental effect upon its endeavours. This is a lack of suit- able instruments. At the last band competition, held in Warrington, it was generally admitted by most musicians present that had . the Buckley-street Mission been possessed of superior instruments they would have made a very bold bid for a prize. Conse- quently the people at Buckley-street intend to make a very bold bid, by having when the time comes round again, a collection of new, and more suitable instru- ments. To assist in bringing this about, a concert was organised in the school room, Buckley-st., on Saturday night, and Mr. J. Taylor, secretary to the Warrington Association of Bands, was asked to preside. In opening the proceedings, The CHAIRMAN', who was received with a hearty round of applause, said he always liked to carry out the old . adage and " speak of his friends as he found them." His Buckley-st. friends had always behaved kindly and considerately with him, and he could, therefore, speak well of them. During the last season of the band's per- formances in the Park, the Buckley-st. hand had been ever ready to assist him; when there came an open date, or if some other band could not carry out its engage- ments, it was only necessary for him to speak to the. leader of the Buckley-st. band, or the members, and they always relieved him of the difficulty by promptly tendering their services. (Applause.) The hand, too, had made great strides in their progressive march, com- mendable improvement had marked almost their every public perfortpance during toe summer, for he could as- sure them that if they could only maintain their perserv- ing, endeavours alter excellence,they would soon become a band of no mean importance in the neighbourhood. (Loud anplause.)Passing for a time,from the consideration of the affairs of Buckley-st., he should like to mention to them what in his mind was a very important matter. It was the object of the Bands Association, if they could procure the requisite amount of public supPort,to create a fund for the purpose of sending each season one of the most prcenising young members of a local band to the Manchester College of Music, and thus help him to corn- mence a professional career. (Loud applause.) Buckley, st. had always been a .friend of the Association, and they possessed some promising players, and he hoped that when their scheme was put into opentioa,Buckley- st. would not be one of the last to benefit by the fund. (Applause.) Much praise was due to Mr. Richardson ad others for keeping the baud together, for they all knew how difficult it was to keep combined a bolsi at- tached to a Sunday school,where so many chaviges often took place. In conclusion he expressed a sincere hope that the concert would yield a substantial contribution to the amount required for the purchase of new instru- ments. (Applause.) The convert, which was a very enjoyable one, was then proceedesl with. The artistes were Miss Lilly and Miss L. Rushton, Miss E. Scott, Miss Jean, Miss A. Mor- rell, and Messrs. T. Hayes, C. Furness, J.Richardson, Ae Pemberton, A. Bailey, E. Sykes, and E. Wright. Th - band also pla) ed several selections. The -accompani- ments were played by Mr. G. Riley, with his usual ver. satility. The -proceeds .realised a very substantial sum

RUNCORN BOARD OF IMPROVEMENT COMMISSIONERS.

The monthly meeting was held at the Town Rail on Thursday. Mr. S. Taylor, J.P., presided and there were present Messrs. F. Wiswall, 11. J. Once, J. M.eadowcroft, J. W. Lightburn, W. Handley, T. Lea, W. liankinson, 1). Bisbrown, E. Imison, W. Leach, J. Dobson; P. A. Lake (law clerk), E. Mar- shall (secretary), and Mr. Barker (surveyor).

lIRAVY INFANT MORTALITY. The number of deaths registered during the past month amounts to 38 ; two of these were inquest cases and of the remaining 36, 3 were due to phthisis, 2 to inflamma- tion of the wind pipe, 4 to premature birth, 3 to senile decay, 4to whooping cough, 3 to heart disease, 6to bronchitis, 2 to scarletina, and one each to ancemia, debility, rheumatism, epilepsy, convulsions, kidney dis- ease, inflammation of iung, rnPmbrane of the brain, and pneumonia. The above 28 deaths show an aver- age death rate for the twelve months ot 22-8 and exclusive of the inquest eases of 21.6 of the estimated population. Fifteen were infants and young children under two years of age.—The Surveyor said there were 45 deaths in the corresponding mouth last year, and the death rate was 27 per 1,000.

HEALTH COMMITTEE. The Health Committee submitted the following minutes—The Sanitary Inspector having reported that nuisances exist at premises situate Nos. 23, 25, and 27, Granville-st., and No. 24, Fryer-st., arising from liquid filth oozing through large ashpit walls and privy walls into yards, it is recoininesileS that notices be served on the owners to abate the nuisances within 28 days of the service of the notices, and for that purpose to remove the ashpits and substitute ash boxes and to substitute water closets for the privies, oue to each house ; it is ' further recommended that in the event of neo-compli- ance with such notices legal proceedin!ss be taken ; that the Isocal Government Board having approved of the proposed payment of £l5 to the Sanitary Inspector for the extra services rendered by him in connection with the smallpox and typhoid fever epidemics, it is recom- mended that a cheque be drawn fur that zunount. In moving the adoption of the minutes, Mr. unison drew attention to the fact that there was no record on the minutes of the action taken at the police court for the closing of Zion Place. It seemed to him that for future guidance they ought to have some permanent record of the proceedings taken.—Mr. Lea said there was no nuisance existing at 24 Fryer-st. Time ashpit was re- built to the order of ties Board two years ago, and was now in perfect condttion.-LMr. Lightburn said if the property had been altered according to the wishes of the Board it was scarcely fair to ask the owners to do the work again.—Mr. Imison protested against Mr. Light- burn's idea. Although the alterations had been made, a nuisance might arise from conditions which did not exist at that time, and it was competent for thit Com- mittee to take further cognizance.—Mr. Lightburn said he had never said the Committee should not take further notice. If the property had been reconstructed in har- mony with the directions of the Board, a further recon- struction was scarcely fair to the owner.—Mr. unison said there was some joint ownership about 24 Fryer-st., and the Law Clerk said the Board could not deal with the Granville-st. property without dealing with 24 Fryer-st. as well —Mr. Meadowcroft said under the.circumstances the Board might bear the expense of the alteration at Fryer-st.—The Chairman: 'that is a dangerous precedent. —Mr. Handley said there was no nuisance at all at 24 Fryer-st., but there was a joint ownership and there was a nuisance on the Granville-st. side.—Eveutually 24 Fryer-st. was erased from the minute, and the minutes were then confirmed.—Mr. Lea explained that he had no personal interest in the property.

RESIGNATION OF MR. HUMPHREYS. Mr. E. VI, ynne Humphreys, writing from 4, Whitehall- yard, London, S.W., under date January 22nd, formally tendered his resignation as a member of the Board of Imprwement Commissioners, and asked the Chairman. to kindly express his sense of obligation to his late colleagues for the kindness and consideration he had received at their hands during his term of membership, and he would lee failing in his duty were he to omit to recognise and formally acknowledge the unvarying courtesy of the officials of the Board in all departments. Their ready and willing assistance had been of immense service to the harmonious and efficient conduct of the business, and the Board were to be congratulated upon having so loyal and efficient staff of public servants. Might he be permitted also to join in the felicitations which the members would, he was sure, tender to Mr. Taylor upon his elevation to the magisterial bench.— The Chairman said for some time Mr. Humphreys had bYan a very useful member of the Board. Had it not been for his energy and ability,he(the chairman)doubtedi if the new bye-laws wottld have yet been framed. A MODERN HOTEL AT RUNCORN.

•In these days of Permissive Bills, Local Option and other measures proposed for the restriction of what is commonly known as the " drink traffic," it is a pleasure to find a house that has been raised above the level of drinking houses into a modern Commercial Hotel. The metamorphosis that has recently taken place in the George and Dragon, in Egerton-street, is complete and satisfactory. A delapid- ated building has been converted into a first class struc- ture, and a house which was used merely by casual callers for a thirst quencher has now accommodation for men and beast in every particular. This transformation has taken place since the house came into the possession of Messrs. J. Jones and Co., brewers of Knotty Ash, near Liverpool, and has been made under the guidance of Mr. Walter Lothian, architeco, of Broad Green, the struc- tural alterations being made by Messrs. Farrell and Son. There is a commodious bar, a parlour, a smoke room and a commercial room, all fitted up in the latest and most complete manner, while the arrangements for the culi- nary department are well suited to the times, and capable of providing everything necessary. There is also a billiard room, admirably contrived, fitted with a table from the establishment of Mr. Thomas Sowerley, of Liverpool, which has cost 112 guineas, and the com- pletion of the house and the formal " opening " of this billiard table was made the occasion of a pleasant gathering on Tuesday afternoon. Proceedings com- menced with a billiard match between two well known players from Liverpool—Messrs. Ryder and Longmyre —who played a game of 750 up before a select company. Ryder conceded 200 points, and a , most interestimr game resulted in a victory for Long- myre by 135 poicc.s. The winner made the biggest break, —66, but he v. as closely followed by his opponent who made 64, including 26 nursery cannons. Both men showed some exceedingly fine play, the manipulation of the balls exciting the hearty approbation of those pre- sent. Afterwards ttiere was supper spread in the com- mercial room, Mr. George Holliday representing the firm of Messrs. J. Jones and Co preside', and Dr. MacKenzie filled the vice chair. Tlv're were also present amongst others—Messrs. David Lothian, Knotty Ash ; Frayer, Liverpool ; John Bustard, Liverpool; I. Jacobs, Liver- pool ; T.Soworby, Liverpool ; —Paverelli, Broad Green ; C.Fotherabill, Broad Green ; —Scragg, Liverpool ; Walter Burnett, Broad Green ; Walter Lothian, Broad Green ; John Weston, E. Tuckey, F. Jolley, J. W. Lightburn,and George Shone. After ample justice had been done to the good things provided, the vice chairman proposed " Success to the House," and in doing so, described Mr. Shone as a man of good principles and sterling-quali- ties, and who would make his mark at that house.—The Chairman then gave the "Founder of the Feast," Mr. Walter Lothian, and described the great transformation scene that had taken place in that house, owing to his professional skill and judgment.—Messrs. Jacobs and Peverilli also spoke to the toast, which was received with musical honours.—ln reply, Mr. Lothian said it was a great pleasure to him to be the donor of the feast. —The Chairman proposed the health of the host. Ho described Mr. Shone as a typical John Bull of the old type, and said that he had not the slightest doubt that under Mr. Shone's management the George and Dragon would become the best house in Runcorn. (Applause:) After the toast had been drunk with musical honours, Mr. Shone in reply,paid a high compliment to the firm of Jones and Co., and said he should do his best for the house by serving the public of Runcora to the best of his ability, and conducting the business in such a man- nor that the most exacting critic of the licensing trade could not find any fault. (Applause.)—The Vice-Chair- man then proposed "Joseph Jones and C 0.," in felicitious terms, and the Chairman responded expressing regret that Mr. Jones was unable to be pre- sent. He said the stuff supplied to the George and Dragon would be of the very best quality, and by being courteous and kind to their supperters,the firm hoped to hold their own. The "Town and Trade of Runcorn" was also given, and several other toasts. The proceedings were enlivened by several recitations,given in an artistic style by the Chairman, and songs by Messrs. Jacobs, Fothergill and Mr. Walter Burnett, who also ably acted as accompanist. Subsequently Messrs. Ryder and Long- myre played another billiard match of 1000 up, Ryder on this occasion conceding 400. Longmyre appeared completely out of form, while Ryder played a brilliant game, amongst his breaks being one of 164, and one of 75, besides several of 40 and 50. Ryder won easily, An exhibition has been arranged by Mr. Shone for Monday, February 26th, when Messrs. Ryder and Diggle will meet at the George and Dragon.

ST. PAUL'S CHURCH TEA PARTY.

The annual congregational tea party in connec- tion with St. Paul's Church. Warrington, was held on Monday evening, about 300 persons sat down to I tea. The tea tables were presided over by the I following :—Mesdames Lawless, Morris, Smethuist, Chantry, Evans, Ward, Lawton. Finney, D. Jones, Reid, Craddock, W. Atherton, Graik, Smith, ,Ilanki,nson, Reynolds, Richardson, Geddes, Chorley, Revnolds,Miss Lvthgoe, Miss Finney,and Mr.Jolley. The usual entertainment followed ,and the proceedings were presided over by the vicar, Rev. H. V. Pigot. In the course ot a short address he said he was very sorry to see that Mr. R. Davies had not arrived. Since Mr. Davies had promised to take the chair he had had a severe cold, and he wrote him saying that unless he was very much better and the weather very much milder he would not turn out that night. Referring to the work of the church, the Chairman said he was happy to say that all the branches of its work were in a thoroughly satisfactory position. The tea party so far had been a greater success than ever and Miss Westwell who knew pretty well how things were going had to report a larger collection of subscriptions than she had ever got before. (Applause.) Their churchwardens were the same as last year, and from all accounts they were all very well satisfied with the way they had behaved themselves. (Applause.) Last year Mr. Hughes and Mr. Forbes were with them, but towards the end of the year the news came that they were to lose them both, and the end of 1893 saw the end of their services among them. They isarted with them with many regrets. The new member of the staff, Mr. Townsend, was very un- fortunately unable to be present, and he had asked him to make his best apologies for being absent. He (the speaker) was very sorry Mr. Townsend should miss this opportunity of making tile acquaintance of sa many Connected with St. Paul's. He hoped they would soon make him feel at home, and get some good work out of him. Their choir was as good now as ever it was. The ringers had done a very great deal of hard solid work d ,ring 1893. They had distinguished the:nsslves amongst the bellringers of the Diocese of Liverpool and held now a very high place. The handbells which were prosented by the churchwardens and Mr. Morris had been of very great service to the ringers. THE HEALTH C631-MITTEE.

This Committee proposes that the council be recom- mended to make an application under the Isolation Hos- pitals Act, 1893, to the County Council of Lancashire, for the establishment of an isolation hospital for the treatment of persons suffering from smallpox, and for the constitution of a " Hospital District," comprising the borough of Warrington and the districts of all adjoining authorities for which such isolation hospital may be made conveniently available; and that the attention of the local authorities of the districts adjoin- ing or contiguous to• the borough be called to the provi- sions of the above act and to the proposed application to the county council.

TEE FIRE BRIGADE.

The Fire Engine Committee recommends that the resolutions passed by the Council on October 26th, 1880, to the effect that in no case the st?am fire engine, er engineer be allowed to attend fires arising beyond the boundary, and that in case of a fire reliably reported outside the borough, the members of the brigade should proceed to the place with manual engine only, be rescinded. They further recommend that in case of a fire without the borough being reliably reported, and a request made to the borough fire brigade to attend such fire,that it should be the duty of the superintendent to- gether with members of the brigade, not exceeding ' eight, to proceed to the fire without delay, and that the steam fire engine " Major " be permitted to attend such fires, and that the charge be £1 per mile outward journey,and not. less than £1 nor exceeding £3 per hour for the use of the engine.

'MIA GS HEARD AND SEEN

AT WARRINGTON.

One of the most wonderful movements of modern times is the Young Peoples' Sojety of Christian Endeavour. Begun on February 2nd, 1881, just thirteen years ago, it has within that short period spread all over the world. At the World's Conven- tion held in July last year in Montreal, Canada, 26,284 local societies were reported with a member- ship of 1,577,040. In the British Isles, the number of societies is now over 1,000 and the movement is growing at the rate of 20 societies per week. As a bridge between the Sunday school and the church it has been found marvellously successful in retain- ing the elder scholars, and training them for Chris- tian work. The movement which is quite inter- denominational has taken root in IYarrington, where there are now several societies connected with churches of various denominations. With a view of bringing these together, and also of impart- ing further information to those interested in the religious training of the young, a local convention has been arranged, to be held in Bold-st. Wesleyan schoolroom onl hursday next at 3 and 7.30 p.m. All the Christian Endeavour Societies Sunday schools in Warrington and within *radius of six miles have been invited to attend the convention which will take the form of a Conference in tbe afternoon and a public meeting at night. Several speakers ex- perienced in Christian Endeavour work will attend, and take part in the proceedings. The chairman of the local committee of arrangements is the Rev. Edward T. Reed, and the secretary is Rev. D. W. Rzsberts.

The Amateur Photographic Society reported a successful year at their annual meeting on Tuesday. They have now 79 members which is a mo,t res- pectable number, and shows that the camera has charms for a good number of people besides the professional gentlemen. Mr. Pritchard, the energe- tic secretary, was desirous of relinquishing his .

post, but his brother members would not agree to it, and he will, therefore, have another year of office. Another successful year is looked forward to, and before the year is out, the Society should have increased its members to a "century."

M rs.II übi-Ne wcom be's concert in the ConseretrAve Club, on Wednesday night, was a great success, the assembly room beiLg crowded, and the audience were well repaid by the excellent programme provided for their delectation. The new song " I'oteen," composed by Mrs. Newcombe, was an ex- cellent Irish song, and well deserves the applause it received. The interest was well sustained through- out the whole of the programme, and whenever Mrs. Newcombe gives her next concert, she may. I am certain,he sure of an equally satisfactory result.

During the past few weeks, a number of gentle- men have been bestirring themselves to form a Floral Society for Warrington. The object of the society will be to promote the cultivation of plants and flowers by competitions and like means. The project has met with such favour that in a very short space ot time,th.e society will probably be an established institution in the town ; and, in fact, a committee will be formed very shortly. A project of this kind deserves the support of everyone who has any love for the beauties of nature, and any- thing to promote the love of the beautiful in a working town like Warrington is to be commended. Horticultural shows are, as a rate, great successes in such towns as Crewe,Preston,and even in Wigan, and there is, therefore, no reason why they should not be in Warrington also.

Almost every week now at the police court, the magistrates complain of shopkeepers exposing their goods for sale outside the doors without any ade- quate supervision, and thereby offering strong temptations to weak minded or needy individuals who cannot resist temptation. During the present week, there have been two of these inuendoes against, the shopkeepers. On Monday Mr. F. Monks expressed the displeasure of the bench at the prac- tiee, but on Wednesday, Mr. John Fairclough went a step further, and threatened that he would not allow costs in these cases in the future. The warn- ings and advice of the magistrates have up to the present been something like pouring water on a duck's back, for the shopkeepers have simply taken no notice of them, but perhaps if Mr. Fairelough should carry out his warning, it will have some effect. One shopkeeper some time ago said that it did not pay to hide their goods, but surely there can be some method found to keep them safe. Various suggestions have at times been thrown out, such as having a net round the goods, or placing them higher up the wall, &c., or that a boy should be employed to keep watch. Whatever is done, the shopkeeper should certainly take care not to ex- pose his goods in a manner to excite the cupidity of a certain class of individuals.

I notice that the "School Music Review" for February has a short article on "Music at Warring- ton Training College." It states that "The Rev. Ntorley Stevenson, the Principal, is an excellent musician and takes a great interest in the musical training of the students. Mr. W. S. Woods is the musical prufessor. The students are of the gentler sex, but male amateur choralists are permitted the privilege of rehearsing and performing with the ladies at these annual events. The occasion is re- garded as a society function, the neighbouting gentry attending in force. A small but thoroughly efficient orchestra is engaged, and altogether the performance is made worthy of the excellence of the music selected. Many hundreds of past students must retain pleasurable recollections of the rehear- sals and pertormances in which they took part,and they must be grateful for the musical culture and high ideals they gained during their student life."

There is a good deal to be said for the suggestion thrown out at the Buckley-street Mission, on Satur- day, by Mr. J. Taylor, secretary to the Warrington Association of Bands. This was to the effect that a local fund should be formed which would enable the Association to eachyear send one of the roost pro- mising of the young instrumentalists of Warring- ton to the Manchester College of Music, and thu.s help him to commence a professional career. It is evident tkat there is a largo amount of enthusiasm in Warrington for instrumental music,and no doubc there are,now and again, young bandsmen who dis- playmore than usual talent. In some cases it is only the lack of opport,initv for development which pre- vents a youthful musician from attaining a pro- minent place in the world of music, and such a fund as that referred to might in some cases provide an opening to a distinguished career.

WARRINGTON CHAMBER OF

COMMERCE.

THE YEAR'S WORK.

The 17th annual meeting of the Warrington Chamber of Commerce will be held on .\londay. The annual report whlch will be submirte.d his been issued to the rneuabers. It contains the fo,lowing allusions to work done during the year.

===== 0002601_18940203_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
BOARD OF CONCILIATION.

At the invita:tion of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council, the Chamber decided taco-operate with them in establishing a local Board of Conciliation (to consist of an equal number of employers and empl)yed) for the adjustment of local labour disputes and the ob- viation of strikes and lock-outs. Each body elected six representativcs,but the proposal ultimately fell through, owing to a difference between the Chamber and the Trades Labour Council as to the constituti,in of the Board. The Council of the Chamber are, however, strongly in favour of some such institution, and trust that it may ultimately be established.

CERTIFICATES OF ORGANISATION. The Foreign Office recently succeeded in inducing various Governments to accept Certificates issued by the local Chamber of Commerce as evidence of the origin oZ goods in cases where such Certificates were rendered necessary bs foreign Customs Regulations. The con- cession has been a great convenience to Warrington manufacturers trading with the Continent, and the Council wish to call the attention of any who may not be aware of it to the fact that Certificates may now be obtained at the Chamber.

LABOUR BUREAU OR REGISTRY. At the ru,gestion of Messrs. 'V. A. Bishop and Co., Borax Manufacturers, Dial Mills, Warrington, t'le coun- cil decided to take the initiative in establishing, a labour bureau or registry for Warrington and the district, pro- vichsd that a sufficient number of local manufacturers could be induced to guarantee the preliminary expenses. A thorough canvass was made by the Secretary, but the general feeling appeared to be that there was not any difficulty in obtaining labour and that the town was scarcely ripe for such an institution. The project was thereupon abandoned for the time, but the council are strongly in favour of the idea, and believe that a complete system of labour registries in all the great manufacturing, centres, working in unison. and kept in touch throngh the medium of a central office, would do much to solve the problem of the uneniplciyed.

BEFORE THE MiGISTR,A_TES.

SUE WAS "CINDERELLA."

At the Warrington Borougn Court on Monday, atberine Conway, a tall, middle-aged woman, no ad- dress, was charged with being drunk and disorderly in jliloreensarket-et. at an early hour on Sunday morning, and further charged with assaulting Police Sergeant Taylor whilst being conveyed to the Bridewell. The evidence of Sergeant Taylor showed that the prisoner was drunk in Horsemarket-st. at 1.10 on Sunday morn- ing and was shouting and screaming. The sergeant went to her and t..ok her into custody, but in order to get her to the police station he was obliged to procure a handcart. With the assistance of P.C. Smith the pri- soner was lifted into the handcart. This mode of con- veyance seemed to please Catherine for she exclaimed that she was "Cinderella." Sergeant Taylor promised to take her to the ball, but at this the prisoner struck out with her clogs, and kicked the sergeant in the face. The magistrates committed her to gaol for one month hard labour, without the option of a fine.

SENOBANT ROOTH RETIRES.

'At the conclusion of the busines at Daresbury Petty Sessions, on Tuesday, Mr. A. Browne, solicitor, said before their Worships rose from the bench, he wished to mention that it was the last occasion on which Sergt. Booth was to fulfil his duties in that court. He thought be was right in saying that he was the only person pre- sent in court who was there when Sergeant Booth com- menced his duties 15 or 16 years ago. They all knew that Sergeant Booth had been an excellent officer, al- ways courteous and kind to all,and on behalf of the bar be could only wish him many years of health and pros- perity in his well-earned retirement, and that be might live comfortably on the pension he would have from the county.—The Chairman (Mr. El. Neild) said he quite concurred with what Mr. Browne had said, and hoped that Sergeant Booth would live long to enjoy his pen- sion.—Superiatendent Atherton said he was very sorry Sergeant Booth was leaving them.—Sergeant Booth said he was very much obliged to Mr. Browne for the kind resnarks,he had made. Owing to an injury he received wet years ago he felt compelled to retire.

WARRINGTON V. WIGAN.

A STIRRING GAMB.

It did not require the presence of a flock of stormy petrels, hovering about the sea of sloppy green sward, at Wilderspool, to predict the coming of a storm on Saturday. The loud uproarious gusts of wind, upon which Jupiter Pluvius rode, and ever and anon swooped down with drenching vigour, was a sufficiently truthful harbinger of the tempest that was to follow. And the fact was patent to the eight thousand of enthusiasts who made their way to the football field t ) witness the contest between Wigan and Warrington. Certainly the issue of the battle was sufficiently important to justify even a common, everyday sort of enthusiast in braving the most inclement of weather, for it meant that if Warrington were victorious they would take the third place in the table of the Lancashire Cup Competition. By the time the teams had assembled on the field, the wind had gathered strength and continuity, and now it howled and shrieked about the grand stand and the pavilion and swayed the goal posts to and fro as though they had been gigantic reeds. It was a well developed gale, of that there could be no doubt. But this did not daunt the players, and the spectators settled themselves down to witness much unprecedented scoring. War- rington had the first advantage of the wind and they came out of it with two tries. But it was loudly shouted that Wigan would augment this when they came before tho wind. Alas—tell it not in Oath !—the Black Dia- mond Hunters did not keep their word—they simply kept the gentleman who iceres,limp with inactivity and ram. But in this half the elements had grown much fiercer. The gale was bringing down sheets of rain and sleet, that swept along the field like clouds and saturated the players through and through, as well as the specta- tors. The players began to carry about on their feet incumbering quantities of soil and slough, and after a few scrimmages it became a difficult task to identify them, so hidden were their features by the clinging embrace of mother earth. The ball slipped about as though it were soft soaped, and the wonder is how War- rington in this second half guarded themselves. But there is no doubt they played well, and the match, des- pite the detrimental consequeeces of the inclement weather, was withal one of the finest and hardest games that has been seen at Warrington this season. But to describe the game more minutely. Barber, that excellent player from Stockton Heath, kicked off, and sent the ball calling on the wind right into touch. A minute later James *Bate tricked Flalliwell, and he too punted injudiciously, and the second minor was chronicled. From the drop-out the oval travelled to Carey, who in quick succession came to grief in trying to clear his burly opponents. A foul by Rigby saw another tall kick going dead,"and then ensued another round of scrimmages and line-cuts, which proved un- interesting to a degree. The visitors rushed the oval by short stages to the local quarters, but their stay was brief,for Taylor made an effort,which was ably seconded by John Bate and Berry. A grand dribble by Rigby gave Wigan again a look in at the home twenty-five,and Carey was floored in attempting to clear. The visitors tried their hand at passing, but the local backs with a smartness which was indeed praiseworthy, upset the sequence; and Potts and Evans leading, Trow was soon reached and grassed, his goal again being jeopardized. Walkden stayed a rush neatly, while the attention of Mitchinson and Barnwell to the Bates did not afford the Warrington backs any opportunity. The visitors re- lieved once more, and after Grundy had charged the kick, Potts upset Winstanley, and the home forwards, headed by rumor, Thorniley and Dakin, again led the way to the Wigan line. The pressure was severe, and though Wigan defended resolutely, they could net with- stand the well-meant efforts of the locals for long, for James Bate kicking right into the mouth of goal, the home backs raced up in a body, and Trow being floored, Carey was credited with a try after the game had been in progress twenty minutes. Evans hit the pests in at- tempting to convert. Following the drop-eut, capital , play by Mitchinson and Roberts reached the centre flag, but Turner started a passing bout which promptly nulli- fied the advantage. The locals persisted in the attack, and Potts accepted from the Bates, but he failed to clear I Webster. The two Bates led the way to the line, and Barber for the first time had a chance of distinguishing himself, tackling Whitehead in true style. The oval was dangerously near the Wigan line, and Jolley nsade one bold bid, actually crossing the rut, but failing to ground. Wigan again cleared with rushes, only to see James Bate return splendidly, but the pass was badly, knocked on. Taylor, Dakin, and Berry effected a clink- ing dribble along the popular touch, and got to within five yards of the line before Trow pulled them up. Roberta saved repeatedly, whilst a rousing cheer greeted a clinking ran by Barber, this smart player gathering from the loose, and clearing a host or opponents before Trow nailed him close to the line. However, from a scrimmage the oval was passed out to Po to, who es- sayed a drop at goal, and the ball failing too rise it can- noned off the post, and Carey racing up scored luckily, Potts making a poor attempt to convert. The visiting forwards got away to centre field bolero being repulsed by Ileesom,and half time arrived with Warrington lead- ing by two tries. The restart was made by Turner, and for a few minutes fast play prevailed, but upon more pressure being introduced by Warrington,this subsided, and for a long time Warrington kept their opponents in check in midfield. It was now that the I rain began to beat down in torrents, and the players soon presented a truly pitiable sight. But the ball was kept riling. A free kick against Warrington afforded the visitors relief, and though Potts met the oval, he failed to gain any ground with his reply. Whitehead made an effort without success. Baroer always accounting for him. Buckley, Thornily, and Berry led the way out of danger, and then Jolley was conspicuous in one rush which culminated in Wigan again

defending. A spell of desultory play followed, and the outcome of tall kicking by Roberts and Winstanley saw Heesom saving cleverly. Evans was to the fore with an accurate tackle to Walkden, but a minute later ironical cheers greeted a huge punt by Halliwell. Following the drop-out the locals rushed down in masterly style, and another glaring instance of obstruction robbed Barber of his customary try. Minors fell to the credit of the visi- tars on renewing the attack, whilst near the call of time the Wigan forwards swept down in a body and looked certain scorers until Heesom stepped into the breach, and a fine save was applauded to the echo. Time was scon afterwards called with Warrington victorious by two tries (six points) to nil. Teams :—Warrington : Heessm, beck ; Carey, Evans, Potts. and Barber, three- quarters ; John Bate and James Bate, half backs ; Tur- ner, Jolley, Dakin, Taylor, Berry, Donohue. Thornily, and Buckley, forwards. Wigan; Trow, back ; Roberts, Walkden, Winstanley, and Whitehead, three-quarters ; Mitchinson and Halliwell, half backs; Wester, Rigby, Unsworth, McNamara, Grandy, Lowe, Arkwright, and Robertson, fcrwards.

WARRINGTON A v. WIGAN A.—Tbe return match between these two champion " A " teams was played at Wigan on Saturday. The Warrington team were deter- mined to avenge that defeat they had sustained earlier in the season, but the elements prevented them from doing so. The wind howled round the Wigan enclosure blowing with hurricane force from goal to goal, and at the commencement of the contest rain began to descend in torrents. Four fifteens were originally arranged, but the vileness of the elements caused the captains to aban- don the game after two of the allotted quarters had been played. Warrington were fortunate enough to have the wind behind them, and ere long were attack- ing the Wigan fortress, a minor being conceded. Taylor then got smartly away, his forwards improving the situation by rushing into the visitors' quarter 3. A long series of packs and line-outs were fought out in midfield in fact the game was a continuous series of packs and line-outs. The ground was also in a wretched state,and the oat husks did not help to improve it, but in the meantime Warrington were attacking, but despite the efforts of the visitors' halves they could not get the ball away to their three-quarters. In the second quarter Wigan soon attacked, but a splendid rush by the War- rington forwards carried the leather to half way. Wigan again pressed, Broady and Massey clearing, and a clever run by Freeth improved the advantage. The contest was, however, fought out on similar lines to the first quarter, but the Warrington forwards were gradually getting the better of their opponents. The referee blew his whistle, the match coming to an abrupt termination and the players scampered off the ground, nothing hav- ing been scored. SALE v. They hid themselves and on looking across the fields saw some men engaged in beating the fields for game. They saw the defendant at the gete with a net ready to catch the game. Wat- kins came up to the defendant who commenced to be abusive. He got his name and address and reported him. The other men got away.—The defendant admit- ted tkat he was in the lane at the time, but said that he never went past the gate, so that he never trespassed upon the land.—Mr. Stuart contended that the gate was on the land and therefore he was amenable te the law. —The magistrates however, dismissed the case. Tal bot.

The order was granted. IRREGULAR SCHOOL ATTENDANCE.—Mrs. N. Stapleton, Walton, was again brought t p charged with neglecting to send her children regularly to school. The case was adjourned at the November Sessions to give the defend- ant another opportunity of sending them to school. As the children had attended regularly since, the case was dismissed. Mr. Jenkins, school attendance officer prose- cuted.

DO(1 OWNERS FlNED.—William Rogerson, Warrington, was charged with allowing his dog to be at large at Seven Oaks, on the 10th January. The evidence showed that the collar was without the name and address of the owner engraved upon it. The slefendan': also gave the officer a wrong address. He was fined ss. and costs.— James Bradford, farmer, was fined 2s. 6d. and costs for allowing his dog to be at larze at Little Leigh on the 21st December.

CHARGE OF WIFE ASSAULT.—Janes Buckley, Mill Bank, Stockton Heath-, was charged with having on the 27th December, assaulted and beaten his wife Sarah Buckley. Mr. Herbert Woods, solicitor represented the complainant, and Mr. A. Browne. solicitor, appeared for the defendant.—Mr. Woods stated that when the defend- ant came home for tea On the date named he told his wife she was drunk. Mrs. Buckley denied the Imputa- tion, and the defendant struck her on the heed and knocked her to the ground. The complainant after- wards picked up a cup of tea and threw the contests in the defendant's face. The defendant used very foul language, and again knocked her down in the passage, nearly stunning her. She afterwards went to Sergeant Booth, and the defendant went to the Red Lion Inn. Two persons were in the house at the time of the assault, Mrs. Backnouse and a servant. —Mrs. Buckley corrobor- ated Mr. Woods' statement. At this stage of the pro- ceedings a compromise was come to, the defend ant agreeing to a separatioa order, and the defendant to have one of the three children, and to pay 12s. 6d. per week towards his wife's maintenance.

EGG TRADE REVELATIONS

1,500 EGGS FOR FIVE SHILLINGS.

Some startling statements were made to Mr. Mead, magistrate, at the Thames Police Court on Wednosday, when a man applied for a summons for assault. He stated that on Monday he bought 1,500 eggs of the man whom he wished to summons for ss.,but when he reach- ed home he found they were putrid. He took them back, but the man refused to return the money or allow anything for them, but instead he broke all of them ex- cept 600, aad the stench in the shop was something fear- ful.—Mr. Mead : Did you examine the eggs before you bought them ? Applicant : No, sir. They are what we call " spots," half good and half bad.—The Magistrate : What do you do with these eggs F—Aphlicant : Sell them to confectioners. Six hundred egns that were not broken applicant took to the vestry and they were con- demned.—Mr. Mead said he could do nothing In the mat- ter. Applicant had no right to buy bad eggs. Hethow- ever, granted a summons for alleged assault, and hoped the vestry would take proceedingt.
