ASSOCIATION.

RUNCORN v. WIDNES.—This match was played at Wid- nes, on Saturday. The visitors won the toss and played up hill, a strong cross wind blowing. From the start Runcorn had all the game, and after a sharp bit of work Douglas scored. After 20 minutes' play the game was abandoned owing to the weather, at the request of the visitors, the horns captain agreeing on condition that the game be called a draw. Arrangements will be made to play the game again later in the seasoa. Runoorn were again without Blooer and Peacock. WARRINGTON BASEBALL RESBRVB v. ST. MARY'S ASSOCIATION.—This match was played on the ground of the former and resulted in a win for the visitors. For the winners Richardson (2) Gibbons (1) were the scorers and for the homesters, Hayes (2) result :—St. Mary's Association, 3 goals ; Baseball Reserve, 2 goals._

„ , • ORFORD CRBSCHNT v. FARNWORTH C.E.T.S.—This match was played at Faraworth on Saturday. Owing to the heavy gale of wind and sleet good play was out of the question, but what game there was proved that if the elements had been favourable the home team would have had their work cut out. Both teams had hard lines in not scoring. No score was made for either club.

WAIIIIINGTON V. ECCLBS.—This important fixture was brought off on the Cricket Ground, Arpley, on Saturday. The Warrington oaptain lost the toss, and the visitors were assisted by the wind during the first half, and scored twice, although the home backs, especially Con- nor and Mather, were playing a fine defensive game. The Warrington forwards also managed to put in a goal during this half from the foot of Norman. On changing ends the Warrington men quickly put on another goal, and then a bard struggle ensued, and shortly before the call of time Norman put on a third goal, the homesters therefore winning by three goals to two. The strong wind and heavy rain prevailing prevented a good dis- play of football.

THE CZAR'S LOVE OF PEACE.

The health of the Czar continues to improve, the lung symptoms having disappeared. The sudden and serious illness of His Majesty caused something appreaching to consternation at Vienna' where (as we learn from the "Times" correspondent) he is "looked upon as the mainstay of European peace." The reports from the Austrian capital as to the Czar's love of peace have not always been so confident, each recurring springtime bringing with it a crop of rumours of the " massing " of Russian armies OR the Galician and Polish frontiers and ominous rumours from St. Petersburg. The "Times" correspondent, however, now says it is generally be- lieved in Vienna that "if we have hitherto been spared the incalculable calamity of a European war it is quite as much due to the disposition of the Emperor of Russia as to the various combinations existing amongst some of the Great Powers." The Czar, we are further told. "conceived a horror of war during the Russo-Turkish campaign. On the field of battle, when surrounded by the dead and wounded, when he had all the unutterable suffering and agony oi thousands under his eyes, he formed an immutable resolve that during his own reign there should be no war if he could avoid it. His strong will has served him well in this noble purpose. None of hts entourage ventures to mention war in his presence." All this would be very satisfactory if we could place ab- solute reliance upon it.

===== 0002601_18940203_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
A REFUGE FOR MILITIAMEN.

The Clerk of the Rolls of the Isle of Man gave judg- ment on Wednesday in the case in which the War Office asked for a mandamus against certain justices of the island who had reiused to issue a summons against a militiaman who had failed to attend the annual training of his regiment in this country. the magistrates holding that as the Militia Act, 1882. was not declared on the face of of it to be applicable to the Isle of Man they had no jurisdiction. The learned Judge went into the cOn- tentiens of bygone centuries on the question of the ,is- land's exemption from the operation of general statutes of Parliament—quoting Coke, Seldon, Blackstone, and the rest of the authorities—and decided that, t‘ no mat- ter what inconvenience might be caused by tbe deci- sion," the Manx justices were right in the course which they had pursued, and that the application of the War Office must be dismissed.

WARRINGTON AND THE ISOLATION HOSPITALS ACT, 1893.

The Town Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle) has pre-: pared a report on the above Act, a summary ot which will be read with interest when the recent small pox epidemic is taken into consideration. The Act does not apply to any borough containing ac cording to the last census, a population of 10,000 or upwards without the consent of the Council. IV enables the Council of every county to provide no isolation hospital in any district within their county for the reception of patients suffering from infectious diseases. Before the County Council will proceed, cer- tain formalities have to be gone through. In the firs place an applieatien by petition must be made by one or more locA authorities in the county. The County Council then consider the petition, and, if satisfied that a prima facia case is made out, they cause a local in- quiry to be made as to the necessity for the es- tanlishment of an isolation hospital. The County Color oil may, however, cause their medical offieer to make an inquiry, and if he reports that an isolation hespiti!l ought to be established they may proceed as if a petl7 boa had been presented. Due notice must be given et the Inquiry. Any Local Authority objecting to be in' eluded within any Hospital District may appeal to the Local Government Beard, whose decision will be conclusive. If the County Council determine upeo the constitution of a Hospital District, theY must then appoint a Hospital Committee,. which will act under regulations, as to election, rotation and qualification which the County Council may make, sod will have all such powers of acquiring land, providing hospital, and managing and maintaining the same a 4 •the County Council may delegate to them. The CouritY Council will retain the power of inspecting any hospital, and of raising money by loan for the purpeses of the hospital. A Hospital Committee will be a separate authority, having perpetual succession and a common seal under such name and style as may be c•,nferred Ott it by the County Council, with power of acquiring is.bu by purchase or otherwise, uithout licence in mortmar• The County Council may invest a local authority sost.h all the powers of aßospital Committee, where the hosPi- tal district is wholly, or as to the greater part, under the jurisdiction of such local 'authority. The nonclud: ing paragraph of the Town Clerk a report reads as f°`" lows You will remember that we approached the County Council on the subject of their providing small" pex hospitals in the country, and that our repres, Mons were made the subject of a report by the Count,' Medical Officer of Health, and that, subsequently, th,e measure which we are now considering was introduce" into Parliament. No decisive steps have yet bpon taken to provide a permanent hospital for the protection of the borough against a recurrence of smallpox. If it is eon' sidered advisable to make an application to the Ceunt.Y Council under this Act, it perhaps would facilitate tine action of the County Council if we invited all adjoining authorities within a radius of say five miles to en-OPo.. ate in the application. In order to secure protection 9ri all sides, it may be necessary to request the Cheshire County Council to apply the Act to the districts adjoin- lug the south side of the borough."

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON

THE REV. J. COTTA.

At the Baptist Ckurch, Earles town, on Sunday night, after the usual service, the Rev. J. Cottatn, pastor, announced that he had accepted an invita- tion to undertake the pastorate of the Baptist Church, at Wakefield. His transfer, however, will nct take place for about a couple of months, as he intends to remain in Earlestewn until the comple- tion of the sale of work in April next.

GOOD TEMPLARY.

The usual weel'ili;es;i;i7of T,l-1-t; Hope of Earles- town Ladge of Good Templars, was held in the Co-operative Hall, Earlestown, on Monday even- ing. Bro. E. Morris, C.T., presided, and there was a good attendance of members. The officers were elected for the ensuing qoarterfaind will be instal- led next session. The programme for the evening was" Tit bits and Scraps," Bro. Wakefield being responsible. Short readings of an interesting character were given by several members.

WARGRAVE CHURCH.

The annual teit—p—ait—y and concert usually given on Shrove Tuesday, in the Wargrave schools, will take place on Tuesday evening next, the proceeds to be devoted to the organ fund. Further particu- lars will be gathered from our advertisment columns.

EARLESTOWN WESLEYAN SUNDAY SCHOOL TEACHERS.

The annual tea meeting of the teachers ot the aoove school took place on Thursday evening in the Lecture Hall. After tea the evening was spent in games of various kinds, interspersed with singing. During the evening the plum cake annually given by Mr. Niolyneux was cut u D and handed round.

MINSTREL ENTERTAINMENT, HATDOCI.

A minstrel entertainment was given in the Hay- dock National schoolroom, on Monday evening, by the Bank-st. (Warrington) Mission Amateur Min- strelm. The programme was of the usual character and the various items were rendered in true negro ..tyle, the humorous songs creating much amuse- ment. Mr. J. W. Jewett efficiently accompanied.

===== 0002601_18940203_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
LEVEL CROSSING ACCIDENT AT EARLESTOWN.

On Monday morning shortly after six o'cloce the large gate which divides the London and North- Western Company's line from Messrs. Evans and Company's private line, at the Haydock crossing was demolished by an engine running through it when closed. The signalman at the spot had opened the gate to allow the engine which belonged to Messrs. Evans and Co. to pass through, but it is supposed somebody must have closed it, and it being dark it was not noticed until the engine crashed into it, and reduced it to a wreck. NEWTON-LB-WILLOWS CHORAL SOCIETY.

The annual concert under the auspices of the above Society was given in the Town Hall, New- ton, on Wednesday evening, before a moderate audi- ence. The programme coasistcd of T. Mee Patti- son's tuneful and simple cantata "May Day " which was fairly well submitted, the soloists being Misses Percival, Kean, Turner, and Grace, Messrs. W.Turne:: and E.Grace. The remainder of the programme coit- sisted of songs,part songs and violin solos,by Messrs. W.Devereux, J.Lewis, W.Lyon, Misses L.Gorse, Per- cival, and Kean. The humorous songs were given by Mr. W. W. Whittle, of Warrington, but we must say, that they were hardly suitable to the occasion. Mr. E. Leyland accompanied throughout in his usual clever style, and Mr. P. Fairclough con- ducted.

EMMANUEL CHURCH, WARGRA:VE.

A musical service was given in the above church, err Sunday afternoon by the choir and organist. There was a large congregation, who thoroughly enjoyed the treat provided. Several of these ser- vices have already been given, in aid of the fund for the reconstruction and completion of the organ, and so far they have been in every way a success. On Sunday an excellent programme was submitted and every item was gone through in a manner which reflected credit on ths organist and members of the choir. The following items were contributed: Choru' s "Behold the Lamb " ; air, "He was dis- pised,"Miss A. Molyneux ; organ solo "The Lost Chord." r. E. SOIREE AT THE MISSION HALL, In connection with the St. John's Sunday school teachers, a soiree was held in the Parochial Mission Hall, Earlestown on Monday evening. About 100 persons were present, and the arrangements were carried out by Mr. Lawless with the assistance of the teachers. Several songs were given during the evening by Miss Matthews and Miss A. Clarke, Messrs. Monks, Duke and Jones. Mr. Appleton and Mr. Week's supplied the instrumental music. Danc- ing was also indulged in,and a very pleasant evening was spent.

INDEPENDENT OUDEF, OF RECHABITES. The monthly session of the local order of the above was held in the Co-operative flail, Earles- town on Thursday evening, under the presidency of Bro. Ratcliffe, C.R. There was a large attendance of members. One new member was initiated, and another proposed. A visitor from Ashton Lodge was present, and gave an address showing that the Order was in a prosperous condition at Ashton, and complimented the Earlestown tent on the progress made during the last three years. The Secretary reported that the next district Council of the War- rington division was to be held at Earlestown.

SERIOUS ACCIDENT TO 1 CARTER AT EARLESTOWN. On Wednesday morning a man named William Okell, living in Legh-st., Earlestown, and employed on the farm of Mr. Alderson, Crow-lane, Newton, was very badly injured by being knocked down and run over. He had been carting some timber to nr. Fitzgerald's yard in Havdock-st.,and whilst unload- ing it, the hor>e started out of the yard. Okell ran to stop it, and in doing so the horse jammed him against the gate, and he fell, the cart wheel passing over his body. He was taken into the surgery of Dr. Valentine„when it was found he had two ribs broken, and was suffering much from shock. His injuries were attended to, and he was removed on the ambulance litter.

===== 0002601_18940203_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
SOCIETY.

The usual weekly meeting of the above Society was held on Monday evening in the Primitive Methodist schoolroom. The Rev. E. R. Davies pre- sided, and there was a large attendance. A paper was read by Mr. E. J. George, entitled "is the patronage of the theatre consistent with the pro- fession of Christianity r —The paper was an inter- esting one, and in the course of his remarks, the essayist said that in consequence of the position of several noted ministers of the present day, the church should have clear ideas, on the question. The question, " What were christi ans to do "? should be answered by saying—" anything that is right," and to the question "is it right to attend the theatre "? he would say "No "!—The argument used in favour of the theatre was that it taught moral lessons, but its supporters never said that it taught spiritual lessons. The great bulk of those places were demoralising. One pron3inent actor had made a statement to the effect that when actors went be- hind the scenes, they generally damned their souls. Habitual frequenters were hardly ever leaders in social, moral or christian work, und the theatre was the wrong place in which to gain moral lessons. The discussion which followed, was taken part in by Messrs. Garnett, Holleyock,Jackson,Hall, Clarke, Goodwin, Toombs, Dale, the Rev. E. R. Davies and Mrs. Holleyock.

FOREIGN MISSIONARY MEETING AT WARGRIVE. The annual meeting in connection with the Society for the propogation of the gospel in Foreign parts, was held in the Wargrave National schools on Monday evening. The Rev. J. Carson, rector, presided, but there was a very meagre attendance. The chairman in briefly introducing the Rev. Dean Scowell, Foreign Missionary, Madagascar, said that the collections during the year amounted to £7 188., which was made up of £4 Os. from the sermons, is. subscription, and the rest from the boxes. Rev. Dean Scowell, spoke at considerable lengt h,aud referred to the work he had accomplished in gett- ing the natives in Madagascar to attend any place of worship. He alluded to the building of the first church there which held about 6000 people, and gave an excellent description of the habits ot the people, of which there were about five millions and of these, three millions were of the Matable or Zulu nation. They had really no God,but worshipped a spirit of evil. While there be explored about 2000 square miles of country that was previously un- known. Madagascar was a splendid country, and if he could get 50 millions of people there to- morrow, they could give each three acres and two cows. In addition to the church of England Society, the Roman Catholic Society, and the french and Norwegians bad all worked very hard there in the cause.

• MISSIONARY MEETING AT NRWTON.

The annual meeting in aid of the Society for the Propagation of.. the Gospel in Foreign Parts, in connection with St. Peter's, Newton-in-Makerfield, was held in the Newton Town Nall, on Saturday evening, January 27th, instead of on Monday, the 29th, as previcusly announced. The change of day did not prove a judicious one, as there was not as large an audience as on former occasions. The pro- ceedings commenced by the singing of a hymn, prayer,and the reading by the Vicar of the amounts collected by those who had held missionary boxes for the past year. The total amount collected by this means realised the sum of £25 19s. 2d.—The Rev. W. Deans Cowan, for twelve years missionary in Madagascar, then gave some amusing incidents from his life among the natives, from the time he and his wife first landed among them—on what he termed their honeymoon trip—when they were to- tally ignorant of the language, until their depar= ture with five children, who, on landing in Eng- land could not ,Teak anything but the Madagascar tongue. At the conclusion Captain Royse, R.N., proposed a vote of thanks to the missionary for his address, and gave a touching story •of a fatal acci- dent to a sailor while their ship was lying off the island of Madagascar. The vote was seconded by Mr. Smith, Town Clerk of Wigan. A collection was afterwards taken in the room amounting to la 9s. 313.—The followiNg day sermons were preached in the church by the above. named missionary in aid of the Society, his text in the morning being Act xiv. 27, and that in the evenine. Isaiah lx. 22, from which, he gave two powerful ialdresses,telling of the difficulties and persecutions the first mis- sionaries to Madagascar experienced, and of the ul- timate triumph of their successors in civilizing and Christianizing a portion of the island. • The collec- tions during the day amounted to £8 6s. 2d.,rnaking with the amount collected in missionary boxes end the sum taken at the Town Hall, a total of £35

RUNCORN WOMEN'S TEMPERANCE ASSOCIATION.

The annual meeting was held in Bethesda LeC,.. tun- flail on Tuesday evening. There was a good attendance. Mrs. Carter, of Bedford, occupied the chair. The annual report was presented which stated that the work of the Association had been quietly yet steadily prospering. Each public meet- nag had been largely attended while the afternoon mother's meetings had invariably been successful. Five afternoon meetings had been held in various parts bf the town and had been greatly appreciated by the "mothers" in each district and at these meetings 24 pledges were taken. The Committee were indebted to the following friends for presiding over or addressing the various meetings held each month during the year: Revs. W. Arrowsmith, J. Simpson, Pulford, Williams, Mrs. Williams (Chester), Mrs. Mundin, Miss Meredith, Mrs. George Marshall, Mrs. Leach, Mrs. Jones, and Mrs. Simkin. The Committee thankfully acknowledge the kindness of Mr. J. T. Brunner, M.P., in allowing the free use of the Union on several occasions. The Committee offered a hearty invitation to all women who were at stainers to join the society,for they hoped that the number of members would greatly increase and that the temperance cause would ever be more and more successful. The financial statement showed that there was a balance in hand of £II 3s. Id. An address was delivered by Mrs. Metcalfe, of Burnley, dealing with the temper3nce question in all its aspects.

THE WHITE STAR LINE AND STEERAGE PASSENGERS.— The White Star Line make the important announcemenc that, with the view of further adding to the comfort and convenience of steerage passengers travelling by the White Star Line, and at the same time conforming to the United States regulations, which prohibit the carriage of feather beds, &c., they have made arrangements to sup- ply all such passengers, in future, with the usual outfit free of charge. The outfit, which will remain the pro- perty of the Company will consist of bed, pillow, blanket, plate, drinking cup, knife, fork, and spoon.

Letter to the 013ttor.

RATIONALISNi.

Sir,—Mr. John S. Howell has again favoured me with his Isotice. In his three paragraphs,he imagines himself to have given reasons for a belief in the "tall of man" from, I presume, primitvie innocence. Now, suppose we give up fencing and come to close quarters? Surely he does not offer that remarkable effusion -his second paragraph—as serious argument? A rather flat attempt at a "goak" belike ! "Words," Mr. Howell, "take up a lot of room (three in your case, Mr. H.) but (if them- selves not per se Mr. H. which makes nonsense of your sentence) prove nothing "—that is if strung together, as you have them in this jumble of assumptions. As a proof, even a presumptive one, of the historical accu- racy of the " fall," he might as well have recited the multiplication table. Our friend, as I have told him,is bungling with unaccustomed tools. He looks out on the world and seeing sin and sorrow there, straightway imagines a " fall" of primitive man to account for it. But the facts of the case go dead against such a supposi- tion,and show rather that man has risen from primitive savagery. Strongly confirmatory evidence of this view is desived from the fact that in the same Oriental alle- gory we have the equally discarded fable of the origin of species by special creation, a fable discarded, even by that illogical logician Mivart. Herein we see the unreasoning jumble in which irrational belief involves itself when it dabbles in, or entertains, subversive facts. One only of the two opinions can he held by any sane head. Either species were specially createsi, or they were not so created. Both cannot be true. Science says species came by natural process—evolution. And this position is strengthened every day,despite the howl- logs and Jeremiades of "believers." I cannot help think- ing a man a humbug who studies science and writes upon it while at the same time professing a belief in the very things his science contradicts ? Such a one must either be humbugging himself or trying to humbug ethers. Mr. Howell would have us believe that he has been an agnostic, but alas! those coldly serene but empyrean beights did net suit his constitution, so, like Joe Bink,in the tale, he "found Jesus." Joe was a Bible smasher, an atheistic species of that genius. Now, Joe's wife "took bad," and was brought round by a doctor who also happened to be a Christian ; Joe followed suit. Had the doctor been a Mahommedan, Joe must have em- braced Islamism !—Yours,

JOHN PEERS.

===== 0002601_18940203_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. II WHITRAM AND SOCIALISM.

Sir,—l have noticed the letter of H. Whitham upon this subject and am constrained to think that the active principle, as in all Socialism, is selfishness. In fact, I have never seen a real Socialist; I have often heard people profess the doctrine, but a little probing has al- ways revealed the weakness of their professions. To those who raise their voices against the capitalists of to- day, I would put this question: Are not the people of to-day as a whole better off than at any other period of the country's history, and is this not the result of the development of machinery which could never have taken place had it not been for the fact tint there were some people who lived below their income? By economy and Care the capitalist, has given the country Vac power to purchase more with fewer hours of labour. We have not to go back many years to find that it took the united efforts of a family to earn a living, girls were put to ser- vice or other work at eight or nine, and boys to the workshop, mines,Esc.,at seven and eight. During my re- Collection, bricklayers were paid 265. and joiners 24s per week in Warrington, and a shilling would neither pur- chase as much of the necessaries or luxuries of life as it will now, when wages are at least 40 per cent. higher. I am tired of hearing people talk as though the develop- ment of capital had been an injury to the worker. It has been a benefit to both. True Socialism should recognise neither king or coun- try, but the whole human race as one family. It must also recognise that the greatest purchasing power of any man's wages, or labour would be the power to purchase an equal amount of the labour of others. lam making no allowance for capital here, taking it for granted the capital is found by the labourer. Now, I would like to ask 11. W. a question. Is it let a fact that taking the whole course of his life ha has been able to purchase a greater amount of the labour of others than he has developed himself? If he had been content to live on the wage of the majority of the workmen arsund him he would have been a small capitalist to-day. I have no regard for a man who has sought every opportunity in the columns of the" Examiner" to run down the cause ef temperance. Under a fair exchange of labour—for labour without any allowance for capital —I am not prepared to ssy the workman of this country would be better off. It must be borne in mind that a day's wages in England will purchase a week's work of many foreign workmen whose labour we consume. Again I shall believe in the Socialism of the individual who says, " the 'value of one man's labour can only equal so much, but as the world appraises my labour at so much above its value, take it, let it be divided amongst those whom the world appraises at less than their value." At present all the Socialists I have seen want their share and as much more as they can get.— Yours truly,

7, Marton-road, Middlesbro'. January 29,1894.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

The defeat which Warrington inflicted upon Wigan ea Saturday, can be considered one of the most vigor- ous " lickings" which they have this season conferred upon any other combination of leather propellers. In- deed, the game can safely be said to have been the most exciting and :most important one played at Wildespool during the season. In the first place the issue of the battle was specially interesting, as a victory for War- rington meant that our town team would take the third place in the Lancashire Cup Competition list. Whilst in the second place a match between Wigan and Warring- ton is always attended by more than ordinary interest, a, Wigan generally prove themselves—to speak in the vernacular—" no chiclcens." With what excitement the match was regarded one may obtain a fair idea from the fact that 8,000 persons witnessed it.

That such an enormous gate should have "turned up" is all the more extraordinary when the wild character of the elements is taken into consideration. A heavy gale of wind prevailed,and swept the field with a drench- ing, chilling, accompaniment of rain and sleet. Almost the whole of the 8,000 spectators would be drenched to the skin before the game was ended, whilst of the players it can almost be said that they were engaged in a water polo match. As the game drew to a close one could scarcely identify the various players, so begrimed were they with mud.

Under such conditions the game was very naturally a forward one, and at times the struggles for supremacy between the two eights grew intensely exciting. War- rington played with the wind the first half, and played a splendid defensive game for some time. After the lapse of about 20 minutes, however, they commenced an aggressive charge. John Bate was the first to make any tangible score ; he succeeded in securing a very smart try, which, however, was not converted. The home men did not allow their endeavours to flag and just before half tinte Carey romped over the line amid a perfect howl of applause.

There were many Wigan people on the field—and some of them were evidently candidates for prophetships. But they were very indifferent—one might almost say poor—candidates. They invariably intimated that Wigan would come out of the second half with some 14 or 15 points more than Warrington. And yet Wigan were content to come out without any points—except, may- be, a few pointed remarks about Warrington and every- thing appertaining thereto.

There was no doubt the winners displayed the smarter combination, and in the last half the Bates simply came out into brilliant prominence. The forwards, however, were entitled to appropriate that emblem of victory— the shirt button. Jolley was the best man of the bunch. Potts, Evans, and Barber also distinguished themselves, and Heesom met his many responsibilities with much credit.

The foll•wing are the tabulated results of the compe- tition up to Saturday :

For. Agit. Pld. Won. Lost. Drn. G.T. G.T. The Warrington Amateur Athletic Club which has only been in existence for twelve months had a very success- ful competition on Thursday evening. The club has made very rapid strides since its formation, and now has about 200 members including all the leading athletes of the town. The large audience on Thursday night must have been very encouraging to the promoters, and it will go to some extent in the direction of making this class of entertainment popular in the town. The various "draws" in the rounds for the competition were vary exciting, the finalists being so well matched that it was difficult at first to choose the winner, and F. Corless had to work hard for his honours. Some of the performances were, however, a little too dangerous, particularly the feat of James Hunt who jumped on the handle of a sword pointed on a child's breast, the child in the meantime lying on the floor. Several exhibitions of the " useful art" were given during the evening, and thosc who take an interest in these things, must have spent a very pleasant evening.

THE CHURCH AS THE IDEAL SOCIETY AND ITS SOCIALISM.

The first meeting of the second session of the Warrington Debating Society was held in the Masonic Mad, Bold-street, on Tuesday evening. The Rev. E. C. E. Carleton read a paper on the above named subject. lie said that when he spoke of the Church he did not mean the Church of England, but the Church at large everywhere and in every age, oh which the Church of England only claimed to be a part. The Church was an ideal society whose constitution was indicated with more or less clearness in the Bible. It seemed to him that at the present day an ideal society was consciously or unconscious- ly one of the cravings of humanity. They wanted an ideal organization, they wanted combination with their fellow men, they wanted united action, some general social movement for the benefit of mankind. The church did not obliterate the classes but united them. It did not abolish the difference between rich ana poor, as the modern socialism seemed to propose by • giving everybody the same wages, any more than it at- tempted to abolish the difference between the strong and the weak by giving everybody the same amount of phy- sical vigour and strength, or the same amount of brain. All these differences the Church lett very touch as they were, while unitine it, members together in the ties or fraternal affectif,n in spire of them. Once more as the ideal society, the Church was a society of voluntary membership. There was no comoulsion cm any to j )in its ranks, and that could not be said of a great in-, nv societies of which they had everyday experience. They all felt that it was a great drawback to the Trades Union; of the country that they frequently interfered with th,-. , liberty of those outside their association, and often heought pressure to bear of a very ohjectienahle charac- ter in order to force the society on unwilline people. Arad the history of almost every st.rike showed that terrorism and outrage were resorted to in order to secure that end. Then the Church was an ideal body as to the Authority by which it was governed. That was, perhaps, the most important of all, ne there ley their security, their freedom from apprehension in j-)in- ing it. The Christian Church made a unique claim up- on-this point, namely, that its supreme Head and, there- fore, its supreme authority, was divine. The dangers from human folly and human infirmity disappe trod. Further, he proceeded to show that the Church bad ideal aims and objects, which modern socialism could hardly be said to have. Modern socialism dealt only with material wants, but the Church considered man as being, made for eternity. Still she did not ignore the import- ance of material things, and dealt with them in her own way. Her method was notso much a method cf organiz t- tion or of philosophic system, but simply the method of instilling a spirit of principles and mYtives into the minds of individuals. He did not know whether these principles as formulated in the Bible would commend themselves to the minds of the modern Socialist any more than they would to the capitalist with whom he waged war. Meny people, he feared, went in for social theories, because it was so much easier to be theoretic- ally generous than to be practically so in their private capacity. They were apt to contract themselves out of. ! their private duties by benevolence of sentiment in public. I They went in for socialistic legislation and got a cheap - • • reputThon for generosity.while they were simply giving I away other people's Money. (Hear, hear.) But thie kind of charity was a sham, and, therefore, such movements when emanating from men whose private conduct threw doubt upon their sincerity must he viewed with suspicion. Let them depend upon it, they could have nn real generous liberal measures forth? benefit of the masses of people needing sympathy and help unless they , were promoted be earnest charitable individuals. The method of the Church was to take care of the individual, and let society take care of itself. (Applause.) Mr.Ntemiin a severe criticism of the paper, contended that whatever socialism the Church contained it was nethine more than mock socialism. Mr. A. Beneerr did not think that the Church was de- mocratic, as Mr. Carleton had contended ; on the con- trary he thoutht it was nothing but the very opposite of that. It ought to be democratic, as Mr. Carleton had said, before it could he really in touch with the people. He (Mr. Bennett) would like to see the present system of patronage superseded by a democratic principle—he WRCLIFFE P.S.A. DOES THE SEED DIE? A PERPLEXING PROB LEM.

Sir,—The other day, on passing the old burial ground 'in St. James-street, Latchford, I stopped to read the in- ecriptien over the entrance door, and was struck with the following quo atson which was taken from the bible, "That whicn thou sowost is not quickened, except it die." The reading of this passage awakened in my mind the memory of a former difficulty—a difficulty— which disturbed my mind many years ago, and as to which I was never able to get, a satisfactory solution. Whenever I put a question en the subj .ct to others who were better informed than myself, I was always assured that it was perfectly true—that all seed put into the ground always did die before it sprang up into renewed life. Well, I never felt really satisfied about this, but my questionines were eventually silenced on its being pointed out to me that Christ Himself had asserted the same thing. I was referred to the Gospel of St. Matthew 12th chap. 24 verse, where I found these words used by Christ" Verily, verily, I say unto you, except a corn of wheat fall into the gr ',lnd and die, it abideth alone, but if it die, it bringeth forth much fruit." From such an authority as this if seemed to me at that time that there was no appeal, and I was obliged, perforce, to be con- tent. But since ther. some strange ideas have come to be formulated about Christ—l refer to the theories about the limitations of His knowl, dge. I have nese!' been able to reconcile these new fangled ideas with what I have always been taught about His Divinity. I am a plain man, accustomed to plain words, and have never been able to understand wisv the same words should not bear the same meaning in the pulpit as in the street. And so, when I was told that Christ was Gad—although in human form—l believed it, although I could aever really ' understand it. But when, in these later days, lam told that in addition to having a human body, he had a human mind—a mind that grew just like other Peoples' —and that his knowledge was limited, just like other peeples'.—l confess to a good deal of intellectual confu- sion and perplexity. And yet I read of an these strange theories in the "Methodist Recorder" of all the papers in the world. I think it was the Rev. Professor Davidson who enunciated them in a series of short articles. Well, I had, in a large me asure, put all this from mind as not being worth an hoot's serines consideration on the part of any sane man, but the whole thine came back to me most forcibly on my reading the inscription referred to, and I tienught : Well, is it possible that notwithstanding their strangeness, these theories are true? That the seed should really die before it could quicken, seems altoge- ther oppos-d to reason and experience. I, at all events, have never known a living thing to die, and come to life again. And in the case of the seed, lam forced to the opinion that the living germ within it never does die—or if if dies, the seed is devoid of any power to grow again, the very explicit statements of Christ and St. Paul notwithstanding. So you see, sir, lam impel- led to the conclnsion that there is something to be said for the" Methodist Recorder" and Professor Davidson, and their strange ideas about the limitations of Christ's knowledge It really isn't so very strange after all—but —and here's the rub—how do they reconcile it all with the doctrine of Christ's Divinity ? Really, I am bothered —hut perhaps the seed does die before it quickens. Will any of your readers enlighten me on the subject? Yours, ke.

HARRY ESMOND.

OTTR LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 3, 1894.

A WAR RINGTON BUTCHER'S CLAIM.

A REMARKABLE CASE. At the Warrington County Court, before a special jury, John Bradbury, butchei, residing at 98,Hoyle- street, Warrington, sued Ann Moffatt, landlady of the British Tar Inn, Winwick-road, for £6 12e. id., alleged to be due owing to the defendant breaking and entering into the possession of premises in the occupation of plaintiff, situated behind the British Tar Inn, on or about the 26th April, and used by him as a slaughterhouse, depriving him of his goods and chattels, valued at £6 12s. id. The defendant put forward a counter claim of 23 for rent, and also a water rate. Mr. Unsworth appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. A. Browne for the defendant. Mr. Unsworth said the plaintiff had carried on business as butcher for a great many years. He claimed that he had suffered damage by reason of the defendant wrongfully entering the premises occupied by him, situated behind the British Tar Inn, without giv- ing him any notice to quit, and wrongfully keeping his goods and chattels which were in the premises broken into. In December, 1892, the plaintiff went to see the defendant Mrs. Moffatt, with a view to renting a build- ing she had at the back of her premises. The defendant agreed to let plaintiff have the building at a rental of 4s. per week, but the rent was not to commence until the plaintiff had obtained a licence for his slaughterhouse. 'I he plaintiff went to considerable expense in fitting up the building for a slaughterhouse and obtained a licence for it in March, 1893. When the plaintiff had used the slaughterhouse about a month, his son on going to the slaughterhouse on April 24th found that a fresh lock had been put on the door and he was unable to get in. The plaintiff then went to the defen- dant,and she told him that she wanted the place for her son. The plaintiff saw her many times to try and come to a reasonable arrangement with her but she refused to see him. In November the plaintiff sued the defendant in the county court for butcher's meat, and the defen- dant sued for rent. His Honour granted his (Mr. Uns- worth) client's claim, and struck out the counter claim. The plaintiff had been unable to get his goods from the slaughterhouse. It was one of the most high-handed proceedings the defendant could do to forcibly take off the lock, and appropriate not only the premises, but the fixtures, and refuse to deliver them up.—John Brad- bury corroborated his solicitor's statement, and said the defendant's son told him,after the lock had been changed that if he went near the slaughter house he would !save him locked up. Browne for the defence denied that the defendant had removed the lock, or had given any instructions for it to be re- moved. When the plaintiff presented his bill in April, he told the defendant that he was giving the place up as he couldn't make it pay, and he was told to take his things out of the slaughterhouse. Mr. Browne pro- duced a key ' which had been used by Mr. Hewitt, butcher at the .slaughterhouse during the plaintiff's oc- cupancy, and given to him by the plaintiff, and this fitted the lock which the plaintiff alleged had been placed on the door.—Mrs. Moffatt deposed that the plaintiff told her he was giving the slaughterhouse up because he could not male it pay, and she told him to take his things, and had never prevented him from doing so. She never ordered anyone to put a fresh lock on.—Arthur Moffatt, son of the last witness, also denied altering the lock or refusing the plaintiff his goods.—Mary Ann Swan- ton spoke as to the defendant saying he had given the slaughter house up.—Mr. Naylor, sugar boiler, gave evi- dence as to lending the plaintiff's son a crowbar with which he sprized open the door of the slaughter-house. BOYS AND BICYCLING.

"In the first place," said Mr. Lacy Hillier, the well- kno wrt cyclist, to our representative, " a boy should have a light taachine,with ruhb•T pedals. Eb, shouldn't try to get one dirt cheap. As a rule, the chewier a naa.chine is, the heavier it weighs. Pay a fair price and ride a good bicycle. Secondly, as to clothes. A boy who intends to go in for any quantity of cycling should he plrticular a hout what lie wears, and his mother should help him in his choice of clothes. Woollen things are'the best. Everything worn next the skin should be male of wool, and then r our young rider won't catch cold after a spin. The same rule applies to all kinds of athletics. Thirdly, al to riding companions. When a lad goes out for a run he should he accompanied by riders considerably older than himself, who will see that he doesn't over-ride him,elf,pr with companions of his own age ; for it is clear that if a youngster goes out with lads who are his seniors, that youngster will strain him- self in trying to compete with, or even beat, riders en- dowed with superior strength and endurance. When boys go out together it's ten to one they will begin to, race. If they are of the same age and strength this won't do them any great harm, hut if some are older than others the young members of the party are liable to do themselves an injury by competing with their bet- ters. I wish I could get boys to see this clearly. Many a lad with sterling cycling qualities in him nips them in the bud by overdoing the thing in his early riding class. Some try to go tremendous distances in a short space of ' time. Of course this is of ry foolish. I don't think a boy ought to do more than twenty-five miles in a day Yes, I think that's about the limit I should put it at"— " Chums."

WARRINGTON LIBERAL CLUB.

RE-OPENING OF THE READING ROOM.

Ar, interesting ceremony took place at the Liberal Club, Bewsey-st., on Tuesday evening. About 50 members of the llub, in response to an invitation from the Committee, met together to celebrate the re-opening of the reading room, after its renovation, the expense of which was defrayed by Mr. 11. Thornton. The room, the embellishments of which we described last week, was first formally opened by Mr. H. Thornton. After the work had been in- spected and admired, the party sat down to a knife and fork tea which was handsomely served up by Mr. Mitchell, the manager of the club. The repast having been partaken of, the party resolved itself into a free and easy meeting presided over by Mr. J. H. Fleming, J.P.,the president of the club. There were also present, amongst others, the following gentlemen: Mr. Wm. Pierpoint J.P.,(chairman of the Association),Councillors H. Roberts, J.P.,Flood, Wil- kinson, and Messrs. T. Birch all, J.P., W. Roberts, J. Tinnion, I. Hayward, J. Clayton (sec. of Associa- tion), T. Pemberton (secretary 'of the club), J. R. Calvert, W. Bibby, J. Albinson, T. Critchley, T. Rath bone and others.

===== 0002601_18940203_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
LETTERS OF APOLOGY.

Mr. T. Pemberton (sec.) opened the proceedings by reading letters of apology from gentlemen- who were un- able to be present. Mr. John Crosfield wrote thanking them for their invitation, but regretting his inability to be present. Mr. A. H. Crosfield in writing regretted having to fulfil an engagement in London, which made it impossible for him to be present. Continuing he said " I hope this ceremony may prove to have been a most favourable augury for the future of our club, and a very favourable sign as regards the party generally. Mr. Chamberlain a few years since was fond of referring to it as a broken and discredited party. He has given up doing so now, and with good reason. As Mr. Bryce re- marked the other day, at Aberdeen, we have been gain- ing as a party, cohesion and heartiness, for several years, and I do not think the difficulty and delays of the last year have damped our ardour and determination in the least. If increased taxation has to be made—but as re- gards the people generally, practically conceded by ex- tending the principle of graduation on progressive lines next'spring—l think we shall be further strengthened, and that we ought to face the further conflicts in store for us with cheerful resolution." Letters were also read from Councillor F. W. Monks, Councillor Shaw Green, Mr. T. Morris and Mr. W. Furness. A telegram was also read from Mr. F. A. Roberts.

===== 0002601_18940203_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
A VOTE OF THANX3.

The President next rose to propose a vote of thanks to Mr. Thornton for the handsome manner in'which he had come forward and helped in decorating the Club. (Applause.) He was sure that the work which had been done was a source of gratification to all of them. The room was one of which they might be proud, for in addition to the beauty of its -decorations, it was a room of some historic note, inasmuch as the Club had been the home of the Beamonts, and other families of note. Those pictures which served as panels had been first painted for the late Mr. Beamont, and were all valuable and cleverly executed works of art. (Applause.) It had been said that Liberalism was dead in Warrington, but if they had required convincing to the contrary, had they not been afforded ample evidence by the generous action of Mr. Thornton ? (Applause.) Mr. Thornton had shown them the way, and it was now open for someone else to step in and assist in further helping the Club. (Applause.) Councillor H. ROBERTS seconded the proposition. He said he felt it to be a privilege and an honour to he called upon to secand the vote of thanks to Mr. Thornton. The Liberal Club had had a very chequered career, and there had been times in its history when the hearts of its enemies had been rejoiced with hope and expecta- tion that its end was at hand. (Laughter.) Liberalism in Warrington, and the Liberal Club in particular,were it seemed to him, very hard to kill. (Applause.) Somehow or other, when things were getting to the worst, something happened whish put them all in good spirits again by the prospects which they never expected a short time•before. The club had done and was doing considerable work for the Liberal party in Warrington. (Hear, hear.) It was only proper that a home for Liberalism should be made as comfortable as it could be made, as far as the circumstances of the party would admit. It was most gratifying to find that a gentleman who had not forced his attentions on the Liberal party, had recognized the somewhat cloudy fortunes of the party, and had of his own freewill and kindness of heart come forward and given his assistance. (Applause.) It I was quite in accordance with the proper fitness of thiags that in connection with the Liberal Club that part of ; the premises that was supposed to supply the material for reflection should be made as comfortable and as handsome as possible. (Applause.) A considerable amount of credit was due to Mr. Fletcher for the efforts he had taken in renovating the room, efforts which he felt sure had been in a large measure actuated by a per- sonal &sire on his part to retain as long as possible the ' works of art which adorned the room, for the interest of those who made use of it. They were particularly obliged to Mr. Thornton f(fr what he had done. (Hear, hear.) They knew he had the capacity to do more for them, and some of them even flattered themselves that he had the intention to do it. (Applause.) Mr. War. PIERPOINT, chairman of the Executive Com- mittee., supported the proposition, and said he did so on behalf of members of the executive, and he thought be was safe in saying on behalf of the whole of the Liberal Association of Warrington. (Applause.) They were all indebted to Mr. Thornton for his graceful act of kind- ness, in making that room look so beautiful and thus enhancing the artistic charms which it had always posessed. (Applause.) It seemed to him that no person could do anything to assist the progress of that club without at the same time assisting the Liberal party. (Hsar, hear.) In what he had dens Mr. Thornton had greatly assisted the club and the party. Then to turn to the workmanship, he thought it reflected the greatest credit upon Mr. Fletcher,who had carried out the work. (Applause.) It,was work which would testify to Mr. Fletcher's artistic skill and be a standing monument to the generosity of Mr. Thornton. (Applause.) In con- cluding his remarks the speaker said there were others in the town to whom they were somewhat justified in looking for some support, and he could not understand why they should hold aloof. '(Applause.) Any defeats which their party had sustained were not reflections upon them—if they had failed to introduce more of their influence into this unfortunate town, well, the loss was not theirs as a party it was the town's. (Applause.) Mr. 'THORNTON, in responding, expressed the pleasure it had given him, to do what little he had done to fur- ther the interests of the Club and the party. (Applause.) He could assure them also, that in the future he should always be willing to bear his share in any improvements. (Applause.) After a long busy life of toillt was a plea- sant change for one to turn his attentions and services to the furtherance of a CRUSE, which he had at heart,for it was a pleasure of this kind which he had experienced in offering his assistance. (Applause.) There were, however, others of their friends who had much money, and he was sure they would also be willing to help if they were asked. In conclusion he expressed himself thoroughly satisfied with the manner in which Mr. Fletcher had carried out the work. He hoped that the meeting that night would be an augury of increased pros- perity for their party. All they needed to do, to secure victory, was to preserve a united and compact front. (Applause.)

LTBRRAL TOWN COUNCILLORS.

Mr. TiNsioN next proposed the health of. the Liberal Town Councillors, coupling with the toast the names of Councillors Flood, H. Roberts, and Wilkinson. In a brief speech, Mr. Tinnion remarked that although the Conser- vatives had a majority on the Council,the Liberal party Could lay claim to a minority which for usefulness and conscientious recognition of their constituents' interests, was far superior to the Tory majority whose conspicu- ous abilities seem to be the spending of money. (Ap- plause.) Councillor FLOOD who was called upon to respond first, said he would have been better pleased had Coun- cillor Roberts taken his place, as he was the leader of the Liberal sectien of the Council. It had been remarked that they were in a minority on the Council. That was indeed so, hut he would prefer to belong for ever to a minority than be part of the present majority in the Council. (Applause.) If his memory served him right, it was 27 rears since the Tories gained ascendency on the Council, and since then they had held sway without intermission. The evil effects which had ensued upon this were not only felt in Warrington, they were felt nutside as well. The town was one of the highest rated boroughs in the county and people would not come here in consequence to open up new industries. (Applause.) The Conservatives had by their influence deposed excel- lent men like Mr.Pierpoint and Mr. Fleming (applause), and in their stead had placed men who were almost use- less to the town, who were simply at the heck and call of the institution in Sankey-st. (Shame.) They were absolutely mechanical voters and recorded their votes in accordance with instructions. But though the Liberals on the Council were in a minority they did their best to thwart the party strataeems of the Tory majority, and he thought they could claim with most of their work the approval of the intelligent com- munity of the town. (Applause.) At such times, however, they wore taxed by their political opponents with resortinv to " low political dodges "—(laugh- ter)—even when it came to the pulling down of a stone wall. It had been said that the Liberal Town Councillors did not often come among them at the club except at election times but on. his own behalf he could plead an honest excuse. A great percentage of his time was taken up by his own work, then almost the balance of the time had to be devoted to the duties which devolved upon them in their civic capacities, so that they had very little time to serve the parte in another way at the club. (Hear, hear.) The speaker concluded by speaking highly of the renovations which they were there to celebrate.

which their reading room had been renov;Ated—(ap- plause)—and of personally testifying his thanks to the gentlt men to whose ETenerosity they were indebted. Councillor H. ROBERTS, in resp mding, said Councillor Flood had termed him the leader of the Liberal 81 ction of the Town Couneil—but he did not occupy that posi- tion and was not sure he would do so if asked. How- ever, he could assure them that it would be all the better if a leader of the Liberal section was recognized in the Council. for it was desirable that in cases where the prin- ciples of the party were at stake there snould be some system of taking united action. THE SHIP CANAL. SHEBEENING ON THE CANAL WORKS. On Wednesday at Frodsham, John Taylor, of 5, Hut, Saltport, Frodsham Marsh, was charged with selling beer, without a licence on January 18th. Yesterday morning the steamer Ohio from New Orleans, arrived at Eastham Locks, and proceeded up the canal at once. She passed through Latchf•rd about five o'clock in the afternoon. Like the Venango, the Ohio is one of a fleet of seven steamships belonging to the Neptune Steam Navigation Company which trade between Baltimore and Rotterdam. She was built at Sunderland by Jchn Laing in 1888, is 293 ft. long, has a 38ft. beam, and is 18ift. deep. Her registered tonnage is 3,600. The cargo comprises 4,300 bales of cotton, and 50,000 bushels of grain. DROWNED IN THE SHIP CANAL..

On Thursday afternoon, Mr.Worsley, coroner, held an inquest at the Walton Arms, Higher Waltsn, on the body of William James Buchanan, a farm labourer, who was found drowned in the Ship Canal at Acton Grange, on the 30th January. Mr. Cooper was foreman of the jury. Several witnesses were called, and the evidence showed that when the deceased was last' seen alive on the evening of the 27th December, at Lower Walton, he was " under the influence of liquor but not. drunk." The night was dark, and the deceased, it was evident, had fallen into the canal accidentally while on his way home. He made use of the road running alongside the canal, which one of the jury said was a most dangerous road at night.—Constable Shatwell said there were no marks of violence on the hody.—A verdict of "Found drowned" was returned. THE TRADE BETWEEN LONDON AND MANCHESTER.

The "Shipping Gazette" says :—Since the opening of the Manchester Ship Canal, the traffic carried by the steamers of Messrs. Fisher, Renwick, and Co. between Lcndon and Manchester has trebled. When the ves.sels ran as far only as Saltport they rarely received more than sixty or seventy tons of Manchester goods for de- livery in the City of London, but now they are on every voyage from the canal loading from 150 to 200 tons of drapery, in addition to large quantities of barbed wire and other goods which were formerly sent by rail. Besides the consignment for London, the vessels are regularly bringing from fifty to sixty tons of Manchester goods for export, the increased quantities having been induced by the fact that there is now no transhipment from the time the articles are handed over by the manu- facturer until they are delivered to the wholesale dealer in London. The railway rate for this class of goods is 255. per ton by rail, while vi 4 the Ship Canal they are carried for 16s. Bd., a difference which on large consign- ments represents a very material saving.

===== 0002601_18940203_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
VESSELS FOR THE CANAL.

A movement has been started at Manchester which may exert a very appreciable influence on the prospects of the Ship Canal. On the invitation of the solicitors to the Canal Company about fifty leading Manchester mer- chants met in that city on fuesday to consider a proposal to establish a line of steamers to trade direct between Manchester and Kurrachee and Bombay. It is said that Messrs. D. and C. Maclver, of Liverpool, London, and Manchester, whose line of steamers to Indian ports has existed a decade, intend adding four new steamers to their fleet. Before giving orders to the shipbuilders, they decided to give Manchester merchants the oppor- tunity of sharing in the enterprise and testifying in a substantial way their faith in the Ship Canal. At the meeting on Tuesday, the merchants were told that if they would subscribe a considerable portion of the capi- tal of the new steamers, thoseoressels would not only be constructed to meet the requirements of the canal, but I that they would be loaded in Manchester, and the cam- pans' would he registered as a Manchester one. A com- mittee was chosen to confer with Messrs. D. and C. Maclver, the details of the project being more conveni- ently discussed in that way ; and the result of the con- ference, with a report on the scheme, will be presented at another meeting.

SATURDAY'S GALE-ACCIDENTS AT RUNCORN.

It was with the utmost difficulty that the traffic be- tween the Bridgewater Lock and the Runcorn Docks was carried on across the Ship Canal on Saturday after. noon during the heavy gale. In ordinary weather small tugs are used for guiding vessels across the canal, but on Saturday the force of the wind was sufficient to drive them from the lock across the waterway' under bare poles." The schooner Cavalier, from Fowey, with china clay, had crossed the canal, and was being guided into the tidal basin of the dock with ropes, when these snapped, and the anchor had to be quickly dropped to prevent the vessel running into the dock wall. A sailor named Robert Johnson had been holding ,a rope, which had been passed over the gunwale, when the enormous strain caused it to come from the stanchion with fearnal force, and striking his arm, made a shocking gash and broke the limb between the elbow and the shoulder. He was attended by Drs. Robertson and Mackenzie, but the injury was so serious that he has been taken to a Liver- pool hospital. A few minutes later the ropes of another schooner snapped, and the vessel came afoul the Cavalier but, fortunately, no damage was done. On Monday, the steamship Lodore, belonging to Messrs. Fisher,, Renwick. and Co. and engaged in the London trade, ran into the Latchrord Locks and seri- ously injured her bowplate. On arriving at Runcorn she was tied up at the Runcorn Lay -by. THE DUES QUESTION AT RUNCORN.

On Wednesday evening a number of the gentlemen who have signed the requisition asking the Chairman of the Runcorn Improvement Commissioners to convene public meeting for the purpose of considering the dues and charges made by the Manchester Ship Canal Com- pany tepon Runcorn bound vessels met the members of the Legal and Parliamentary Committee in the Town Hall. The chair was occupied by Mr. J. Pritchard. and there was a good representative attendance. It was de- cided to appoint a committee to obtain all possible in- formation on the subject, and to embody the same in a report to be submitted to a fature meeting, so that a sabime could be prepared in readiness for presentation to the directors of the Ship Canal Company. THE ASSESSMENT OF THE SHIP CANAL.

At the tnonthiy meeting of the Warrington Assess- ment Committee, held yesterday, the Rev. G. F. Roberts presided. The Clerk (Mr. J. C. Sutton), read a report relative to the assessing of the Manchester Ship Canal. The report stated that a meeting of Clerks to the Assess- ment Committees, connected with townships adjoining the Ship Canal, had been held at the Lion Hotel, on the 18th January and the question of assessing the Canal undertaking was discussed a resolution was adopted to the following effect: That the Manchester Ship Canal cannot be satisfactorily assessed without a valuation of the undertaking as a wholel and that such valuation be made by the most competent valuers that can be ob- tained. Also, that it be an instruction to all valuers to apportion their valuations in proportion to their respective interests between the several unions atui the several townhipa in such unie.O.S4thlOAgh which

the canal passes, and that the several unions contribute to the costs of such valuation according to the amount of the valuation thrown on each. That in the event of tile Canal Company appealing against the assessments of any townships, based on such valuation as before mentioned, the costs of defending the said townships against such appeals be paid by the unions involved, so far as the law would allow, and in proportion to the rateable value of the said unions. The resolution further advised that no valuation be made of the canal for 12 months, and that in the meantime the Canal Company be charged the amount which they have been paying to make up the deficiency of the rateable value of each township, by reason of their tak- ing land for the construction of the Canal. Another clause in the resolution advocated the formation of a Committee which should endeavour to make some ar- rangement with the Ship Canal Company. The meeting was adjourned until thel3th of February.—The authority expressed themselves in favour of the terms of the re- solution, and it was decided that Mr. J. C. Sutton attend the adjourned meeting, accompanied by the Chairman, the Rev. G. F. Roberts.

===== 0002601_18940203_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
DETAILS OF THE DEPOSITED BILL.

Under the bill which will be promoted next session by the Manchester Ship Canal Company, power is sought to construct a pier, about 600 yards in length, commencing on the seaward side and at the north-western end of the shortest of the entrAnce locks at Eastham. rower is also sought to construct a short railway in Runcorn, 6 fur- longs 6.5 chains in length, commencing at the boundary between the townships of Acton Grange and Moore and terminating by a junction with the Birkenhead, Lanca- shire, and Cheshire Junction Railway. The bill also proposes to confirm the construction of two jetties al- ready constructed from the southern and northern walls of Weston Marsh lock, and revives the expired powers granted in 1885 for the construction of a railway at War- I rington, commencing by a junction with a branch rail- way of the London and North-Western Railway and terminating in the Arpley Meadows. The preamble of the bill states that the greater part of the lands necessary for this railway had been acquired before the powers ex- pired. By clause 18 the Company ask to be empowered to appoint and license persons to act as pilots for navi- gating, conducting, and moving vessels within the canal I docks and basins of the Company and the approaches thereto, and to subject every person acting as pilot without a licence to a penalty of £5. By clause 19 power is sought to enable the Company to make by-laws with respect to (1) the carrying of animals imported from any country beyond the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland, (2) the conduct of persons navigating or superintending such vessels, (3) the flying of a distin- guishing flag by all vessels having any such animals on board until such animals have been disembarked. Clause 20, upon which considerable opposition is expected, pro- poses to enact that "no vessel leading fir discharging any part of its cino in or at any part of the canal docks or basins or at any quay, wharf, pier, jetty, or work of the Company shall be liable to the payment of the wharf rate mentioned ia section 245 of the Mersey Docks Acts Consolidation Act, 1858, by reason of any portion of the cargo of such vessel being brought into or on any pier, landing-stage, quay, or premises of the Dock Board." Clause 21 proposes to exempt the canal from the pro- visions of the Railway and Canal Traffic Act, 1888, and by clause 23 power is sought to demand "reasonable rates and charges" for goods stored on any vessel or floating warehouse in the harbour and port of Man- chester, or in any timber ponds, or in or on any premises of the Company. Power is also sought under this bill' to supply water to vessels at "a reasonable charge," and penalties not exceeding £5O are sought to be inflicted on any person who casts any solid matter-into the canal, in addition to the cost of dredging for and removing such matter.

THE PR9POSED GRAVING DOCK FOR RUNCORN. At a meeting of the Runcorn Improvement Commis- sioners, on Thursday, Mr. Bisbrown introduced the subject of a graving dock which the Manchester Ship Canal Company had promised to provide for Runcorn. He stated that in March 1890, an agreement was made between the Commissioners and the Ship Canal Company to the effect that within twelve months of the opening of the canal a graving dock should be provided at Old Quay. It was an important matter to a large body of workieg- men in the town. Before the commencement of the Ship Canal there were three graving docks en the river side, viz., Castle Rock yard, Belvedere yard, and the Old Quay yard. After the channel was commenced these yards were closed, and the men were scattered in all directions. This affected a very important industry in the town, and he thought the Board should request the directors of the canal to take into consideration the provision of a dock without delay. He did not think that because they proposed providing it within twelve months, that they should not do so before that time had expired. If they could do it at once they would confer a benefit upon the inhabitants of the town, in which the ; trade at the present time was so bad. There was a I strong feeling among shipwrights respecting the matter. They had no desire to dictate to the Canal Company, they simply wishing to request them to take the impor• taut matter into thuir consideration at the earliest possi- ble moment. 1a bay Crnes there used to be something like 200 mechanics employed in the yards named. He would move that they write the Ship Canal Company, pointing out that the ship and boat building trade had been injuriously affected by the Ship Canal, and respect- fully urge them to push ferward with the graving dock. They had at the present time no provision for repairing ships. Be believed that the directors of the canal were honourable men, and that if the case were brought before them they would take some notice of their appeal.—Mr. Lightburn said they all felt the necessity of retaining for Runcorn so important a branch of trade as that of ship- building. They had obtained a clause for the dock, and if anything could be done to hasten the work, he would only be too pleased. He would, therefore, suggeat to the mover of the proposition that the subject be referred to the General Purposes Committee, in order that the whole thing might be thoroughly sifted.—Mr. Bisbrown con- sidered that the suggestion thrown out by Mr. Lightburn would be the wisest one, and therefore he had pleasure in moving that the subject be referred to the General Purposes Committee.—Mr. Lightburn seconded, and the motion was carried.

WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL.

SPECIAL MEETING.

- • A special meeting of the members of the Town Council was held at the Tcwn Hall on Monday evening, Alderman Holmes in the chair. The Press were excluded from the meeting.

THE TOWN HALL WALL. The question of the proposed alterations in front of the Town Hall was one of the matters considered, and after a long discussion it was decided that the Town Hall, Park, and Baths Committee be authorized to spend a sum not exceeding £550 for the removal of the wall be- tween the two existing gates and the laying out of the land, and to substitute an ornamental iron palisading with the central gates presented to the town by Mr. F. Monks J.P. A member made a proposition to the effect that the whole improvement be proceeded with at once, namely, that the wall should be demolished from the I entrance near Mr. Riley's office to Crosfield-st. This re- ceived the support of several other members, but ulti- mately, on the plea that the committee were .short of fends, the first-named scheme was agreed to, on the understanding that the improvement up to Crosfield-st. shall be carried out as soon as practicable.

A PROPOSED INCREASE OF SALARY. The Sanitary Works Committee have decided to re-- commend the Town Council to increase the saiary of Mr. James Deas, the manager of the Longford Del. 6-,, from £2OO to £3OO. This was brought forward for (: msidera- tion by Councillor Monks, but many of the me:nbers had then left the room, and there was no general ex- pzession of opinion with regard to the proposed increase. Councillor Monks submitted a statement showing the cost of the Longford Department for the two years pre- ceding, and for one year after Mr. Drag took up the- management, from which it would appear that the cost of working the department in 1890-91 was £2,167, in- -1891-92, £3,252, and in 1892-93, under Mr. Deas's management, £B7l. This extraordinary decrease is, of course, attributed to Mr. Deas's good management_ Mr. Deas has also increased the output by 100 tons per annum, which, at £5 a ton, represents an increased in- come of £5OO. He has also effected a considerable sav- ing in the cost of fuel. Formerly £2OO a year was spent, ,on fuel, but now one ton a week is made to suffice. The I wages paid are also less, although the machinery and, plant have been increased, and there is now no night work at the depot. It is under Mr. Deas's management that the system of collecting night soil has been changed from a day into a night collection, an improvement which the public generally most appreciate.

THE UNEMPLOYED, The unemployed in Warrington was the next question brought forward for consideration. Last week a deputa- tion waited upon the Town Clerk, when it was repre- sented to him that upwards of 400 people were still out. of employment. The deputation asked that the Corporation should provide work for them. Several of the members expressed the opinion that the statements made on behalf ot the unemployed had been greatly exaggerated, and that there was not sufficient distress in the town to warrant the interference of the Corporation. It wls decided not to take any steps in the matter.

THE PASSENGER TRAFFIC AT RUNCORN.

Mr. Leach at the same ta.eeting said that another matter for consideration was the provision of a landing stage at Runcorn, in connection with the passenger traffic on the canal. It was likely that a number of persons would travel between Manchester and Liverpool along the undertaking, and it might be that some of them would desire to leave the vessel, or others might wash to get on board at Runcorn. It would be well, in his opinion, if they could persuade the Ship Canal Com- pany to provide a landing etage.—Mr. Meadowcroft : Perhaps they will want us to guarantee the number of passeisgers. (Laughter.)—Mr. Grice thought that the company ought to provide means for passengers entering or leaving vessels at Runcorn.—This subject was also re- ferred to the General Purposes Committee. RUNCORN PILO TS AND THE SHIP CANAL.

The Liverpool pilots, who form a somewhat ex- clusive body are making strenuous endeavours to capture the sole right to take vessels un the Ship Canal to Manchester. Great stress is laid upon the fact that other men engaged in navigating ships to the new seaport, are not licensed, and it, has been stated in the Liverpool press that they are little better than Mersey watermen. It is true that the Runcoin pilots are not licensed, but in addition to being a highly respectable body of men, they are, with one or two exceptions, ex-captains of schooners or steamers. Several of them have traded to foreign ports. The sta'ement that the " unlicensed men "are little better than Mersey watermen is thus too sweeping, and is certainly void of that accuracy which is usually ex- pected when technical matters are under discussion. In an interview, on Saturday, one of the oldest and most experienced pilots of Runcorn saia it was extraordinary that the Liverpool pilots should be displaying so much eagerness to obtain a monopoly of the Ship Canal pilot- age, where previously they have resolutely declined to lace the narrows and the currents of the Upper Mersey. The course of the deep from the Sloyne to Runcorn is most tortuous, and the channel to be navigated is in many places no wider, nor so wide, as the Ship Canal, while the currents are very peculiar,. and it says much for the skill and j4idgment of the Run- corn pilots that accidents so seldom occur. The ships might not be very large ones, but it was no mean feat to successfully bring vessels carrying 700, 800, or 900 tons lup to Weston Point with only a foot or two of water to spare. This experience, said the Runcorn pilot, had stood theta in good stead in navigating the Ship Canal, and there had only been one accident to a ship piloted by a Runcorn pilot since the waterway was opened: an in' that case The accident occurred through other causes. The statement that the only ship that passed up, the canal on the opening day "without casualty of any kind was that which chanced to be in charge of a licensed pilot" in fatly denied. About seven Runcorn pilots were in charge of ships on that day, and not one met with the slightest accident. It was a Runnorn pilot who was in eharge of ene of the biggest steamships, in the procession, the Edith Hough, and since that time Run- oorn pilots have taken some of the largest ships to Man- chester. "It is not the licence that makes the pilot," said the ex-captain interviewettl, "and I claim that the Runcorn men are quite as qualified and as practirally capable of taking a ship to Manchester as any licensed man from the seaport of Liverpool. All we ask for is a fair field and no favour, and if the Manchester Ship Canal do ultimately license pilots, I hope they will give the Runcorn men a chanee in the competition." A Liverpool man in conversation with our own reporter, a few days ago, stated that a great deal too much was being made of the accidents on the Ship Canal. Not a day passed scarcely, he said, but some vessel sustained damage at the Liverpool docks, and often of a far more serious character than any yet recorded on the Ship Canal. As the man in question is cooneeted with the shipping industry, his word is worth of weight. It is quite clear that the same look out is not kept for acci- dents at Liverpool docks as exists oa the banks esa the new watesvray.
